                - Indian Newspapers (Incitement to Offences) Act, 1908:  
                    - Targeted newspapers publishing content inciting violence or rebellion.  
                    - Allowed confiscation of printing presses and penalization of publishers.  
                    - Suppressed nationalist journalism and free speech.  
                - Criminal Law Amendment Act, 1908:  
                    - Declared certain organizations as unlawful for anti-government activities.  
                    - Empowered the government to detain individuals without trial.  
                    - Used against extremist nationalist groups and revolutionary societies.  
                - Indian Press Act, 1910:  
                    - Imposed heavy security deposits on newspapers critical of the government.  
                    - Allowed seizure of newspapers publishing seditious material.  
                    - Further tightened control over the press after earlier laws proved ineffective.          
            - Extremist Leaders' Fate:
                - Tilak: Jailed in Mandalay (Burma) for 6 years.
                - Aurobindo and B.C. Pal: Retired from active politics.
                - Lajpat Rai: Left for abroad.
            
    - First World War and Nationalist Response in India
        - Home Rule League Movement
            - Overview of the Home Rule League Movement
                - The Home Rule Movement emerged as India's response to the First World War, reflecting a growing demand for self-governance.
                - It was inspired by the Irish Home Rule Leagues and marked the rise of a more aggressive approach to Indian politics.
            - First World War (1914-1918)
                - When the First World War began, the Moderates chose to support British war efforts, seeing it as both a duty and a strategic move to negotiate concessions later.
                - The Extremists disagreed with this approach, but Bal Gangadhar Tilak took a unique stance. He pledged loyalty to the British Crown and encouraged Indians to assist Britain during its time of need, hoping this support would yield political gains.
            - Opinion of Industrialists
                - Wealthy Indian industrialists viewed the war as a chance to profit. The conflict disrupted British industrial production, creating shortages that increased demand for goods from Indian industries.
                - This economic boost fueled the growth of Indian industries, and industrialists wanted to maintain this momentum after the war. They were eager to organize and back nationalist movements to protect their interests.
            - Reasons for the Home Rule League Movement (HRLM - Why?)
                - Some nationalists believed that only popular pressure could force the British government to grant political concessions.
                - The Moderates grew disillusioned with the limited reforms of the Morley-Minto Reforms (1909), which failed to meet their expectations.
                - Ordinary people were suffering from wartime hardships, including high taxes and rising prices, making them ready to join aggressive protests.
                - The war exposed the hypocrisy of imperialist powers. As major nations fought each other and spread propaganda, the myth of white superiority weakened, inspiring Indians to challenge British rule.
                - Tilak, released from prison in June 1914, was prepared to lead. He reassured the government of his loyalty and signaled to the Moderates that he sought administrative reform, not revolution, similar to the Irish Home Rule model.
                - Annie Besant, an Irish theosophist living in India since 1896 and president of the Theosophical Society, decided to expand her work by launching a movement for Home Rule, modeled on the Irish example. She later became INC president in 1917.
                - Background on Theosophical Society: Founded in 1875 by H.P. Blavatsky and Colonel Olcott in New York, it established its first headquarters in Adyar, near Madras, in 1882.
            - Objectives of the Movement (What?)
                - The Home Rule League campaign aimed to educate the common person about home rule, presenting it as self-government within the British Empire.
            - Methods of the Movement (How?)
                - The movement sought to achieve its goals through widespread political education and engagement. 
                    - This included holding public meetings, setting up libraries and reading rooms with books on national politics, organizing conferences, conducting classes for students on political issues, and spreading propaganda through newspapers, pamphlets, posters, illustrated postcards, plays, and religious songs. 
                - It also involved collecting funds, organizing social work, and participating in local government activities.
            - Composition of the Leagues
                - Both Tilak and Besant understood that success required the support of the Moderate-dominated Indian National Congress (INC) and full cooperation from the Extremists.
                - After failing to bridge the Moderate-Extremist divide at the 1914 INC session, Tilak and Besant chose to launch their own political initiatives.
                - By early 1915, Annie Besant began campaigning for post-war self-government, inspired by white colonies like Canada and Australia. She used her newspapers, *New India* and *Commonweal*, and organized public meetings and conferences.
                - At the 1915 INC session, Tilak and Besant's efforts gained traction. The Congress agreed to admit Extremists, committed to educative propaganda, and revived local-level Congress committees.
            - Tilak's League
                - Established in April 1916 during the Bombay Provincial Conference in Belgaum, with G.S. Khaparde as a founding member.
                - Operated in Maharashtra (excluding Bombay city), Karnataka, Central Provinces, and Berar, with six branches.
                - Demands included Swarajya (self-rule), formation of linguistic states, and education in vernacular languages.
                - Tilak famously declared, “Swaraj is my birthright and I shall have it,” during this agitation.
                - In his speeches, Tilak avoided religious appeals, opposed untouchability, and urged Brahmins to tolerate non-Brahmins. He stated, “If God were to tolerate untouchability, I would not recognize him as God at all.”
                - The British government issued Tilak a show-cause notice, but the High Court cleared him, giving the Home Rule Movement legal legitimacy. By 1917, Tilak's league had 14,000 registered members.
            - Besant's League
                - Launched in September 1916 in Madras, covering the rest of India, including Bombay city.
                - Had 200 branches, loosely organized compared to Tilak's League. George Arundale served as organizing secretary, and Sir S. Subramaniam Aiyer (Grand Old Man of South India) was honorary president.
                - Earlier in 1916, impatient followers like Jamnadas Dwarkadas, Shankerlal Banker, and Indulala Yagnik set up Home Rule groups, started the Bombay newspaper *Young India*, and created an All India Propaganda Fund for pamphlets.
                - Key figures like Motilal Nehru, Jawaharlal Nehru, Bhulabhai Desai, Chittaranjan Das, Madan Mohan Malaviya, Mohammad Ali Jinnah, Tej Bahadur Sapru, and Lala Lajpat Rai later joined, some leading local branches. Joseph “Kaka” Baptista was another prominent Bombay activist.
            - Government Response
                - The government responded with harsh repression, especially in Madras, where students were banned from political meetings.
                - In June 1917, Annie Besant, B.P. Wadia, and George Arundale were arrested. In protest, Sir S. Subramaniya Aiyar renounced his knighthood, and Tilak called for passive resistance.
                - Annie Besant was released in September 1917.
            - Shortcomings and Fade-Out
                - The movement lacked effective organization.
                - Communal riots in 1917-18 weakened its momentum.
                - Moderates were pacified by Montagu's 1917 statement promising reforms and Besant's release.
                - Extremists' talk of passive resistance from September 1918 distanced Moderates.
                - The Montagu-Chelmsford Reforms, announced in July 1918, further divided nationalists.
                - Leadership weakened: Tilak went abroad in September 1918 for a legal case, while Besant wavered on reforms and passive resistance strategies.
            - Contributions
                - By amplifying the Moderates' political education program, the movement gained support from many Moderates. Members of Gokhale's Servants of India Society, though barred from joining, actively promoted Home Rule nationwide.
                - It shifted focus from the educated elite to the masses, altering the nationalist movement's trajectory away from the Moderate path.
                - Established organizational links between urban and rural areas.
                - Inspired a generation of passionate nationalists.
                - Prepared the masses for Gandhian-style politics.
                - Tilak and Besant's efforts led to a Moderate-Extremist reunion at the 1916 Lucknow Session, revitalizing the Congress as a tool of Indian nationalism.
                - Post-1916 Congress session, a joint meeting of both leagues addressed over 1,000 delegates under one canopy.

        - Lucknow Session of the Indian National Congress, 1916
            - Readmission of the Extremists to Congress
                - Presided over by Moderate Ambika Charan Majumdar, the session welcomed back Extremists led by Tilak.
                - Facilitated by the deaths of staunch Moderate opponents Gopal Krishna Gokhale and Pherozshah Mehta in 1915.
                - Tilak's League organized a “Congress Special” or “Home Rule Special” train to transport Western India delegates to Lucknow, starting a new Congress tradition.
                - Tilak's proposal for a Congress Working Committee (to handle daily operations and implement resolutions) was rejected.
            - Lucknow Pact Between Congress and Muslim League
                - Opposed by Madan Mohan Malaviya.
                - The Muslim League agreed to joint constitutional demands with Congress, accepting separate electorates until any community requested joint ones.
                - Muslims received a fixed proportion of seats in all-India and provincial legislatures.
            - Joint Demands
                - Expand representative assemblies at central and provincial levels with elected majorities and greater powers.
                - Set a 5-year term for the Legislative Council.
                - Ensure half the members of the Viceroy's and provincial governors' executive councils were Indian.
            - Montagu's Statement (August 1917)
                - Government policy aimed at “increasing participation of Indians in every branch of administration and gradual development of institutions with a view to the progressive realization of responsible government.”

        - Nationalist Resurgence After the War
            - Post-War Economic Hardships
                - Industry
                    - Rising prices followed by a recession, coupled with increased foreign investment, pushed many Indian industries toward closure. They demanded protection from imports and government aid.
                - Workers and Artisans
                    - Faced unemployment and struggled with high prices.
                - Peasantry
                    - Burdened by high taxes and poverty, peasants awaited leadership to protest.
                - Soldiers
                    - Returning from foreign battlefields, they shared experiences with rural communities, shocked by India's poverty and reduced liberties.
                - Educated Urban Classes
                    - Suffered unemployment and were acutely aware of British racism.
            - Expectation of Political Gains for War Cooperation
                - India's contribution to the British war effort was immense, with nationalists offering support and countless troops sacrificing their lives.
            - Nationalist Disillusionment with Imperialism Worldwide
                - Allied powers promised democracy and self-determination to colonial allies during the war, but post-war actions betrayed these pledges.
                - Wartime propaganda exposed colonial misdeeds, yet the Paris Peace Conference and treaties showed imperialist powers intent on retaining and redistributing colonies, not freeing them.
                

- Gandhiji Before 1915
    - Introductory Note
        - Message by Kawal Sir
            - From an exam perspective, detailed knowledge of Gandhi's life before 1915 isn't critical. However, understanding his South African experiences is key to grasping his philosophy and methods, which shaped his role in India's independence struggle. Learning history this way turns it into a compelling story.
            - Feel free to skip this section if time is short!

    - Gandhi Before India
        - Early Life
            - Born on October 2, 1869, in Porbandar, Kathiawar, Gujarat, into a well-to-do Vaishya family deeply influenced by Jainism, where ahimsa (non-violence) is a core principle.
            - In 1881, at age 12, he went to England to study law. He returned after 10 years as a young barrister and began practicing at the Bombay High Court.
            - Struggling to build a successful practice in Bombay, he moved to Rajkot, earning a living by drafting petitions.
        - Move to South Africa
            - In 1893, at age 24, Gandhi traveled to Durban in Natal Province, South Africa, for a one-year contract related to a legal case for his client, Dada Abdullah. He was the first Indian barrister to arrive there.
            - He faced racism and humiliation, notably when, despite holding a first-class ticket, he was thrown off a train to Pretoria after a white passenger objected to sharing the compartment with an Indian.
            - Intended as a brief (short and concise) stay, he remained in South Africa for 20 years, dedicating himself to organizing Indian workers to fight for their rights.

    - Conditions of Indians in South Africa
        - Indians faced severe racial discrimination, denied citizenship rights, freedom to trade, and property ownership.
        - They were barred from voting, restricted to specific residential areas, and prohibited from using footpaths or leaving home at night without permits.
        - First- and second-class railway compartments were off-limits to them, and all Indians were derogatorily (showing a lack of respect) labeled “coolies,” meaning laborers.

    - First Phase: Moderate Phase of Struggle (1894-1906)
        - Struggle Against Disenfranchisement Bill
            - Gandhi opposed the Disenfranchisement Bill, relying on petitions and memorials sent to South African and British authorities. He believed that informing them of the Indians' plight would prompt action, as Indians were British subjects.
            - He founded the Natal Indian Congress to unite diverse Indian groups and launched the newspaper *Indian Opinion* to amplify their voice.
            - This organization led non-violent protests against the oppressive treatment of Indians and native Africans by white authorities.
        - Early Actions
            - In 1896, Gandhi briefly visited India, recruiting 800 Indians to join him in South Africa. On their arrival, an angry mob attacked them, injuring Gandhi. He exercised self-restraint, marking the start of his philosophy of winning over opponents with peaceful resistance. This approach paid off, as British pressure forced the abandonment of the disenfranchisement effort.
            - During the Boer War (1899), Gandhi organized the Natal Indian Ambulance Corps with 1,100 Indians to aid the British, despite ongoing discrimination against Indians.
        - Connection with Indian National Congress (INC)
            - In 1901, Gandhi attended the Calcutta INC session (presided by W.E. Wacha), his first contact with the organization. He successfully passed a resolution highlighting the plight of Indians in South Africa.
        - Leadership and Lifestyle
            - By 1903, Gandhi was the recognized leader of South Africa's Indian community, living a disciplined life of self-restraint.
            - Inspired by John Ruskin's *Unto This Last*, which praised rustic living, Gandhi established Phoenix Farm near Durban. Here, he trained cadres in non-violent Satyagraha—peaceful defiance of unjust laws, mass arrests, occasional hartals (economic shutdowns), and bold marches—fostering a fearless spirit against repression. He also published *Indian Opinion* from the farm.

    - Second Phase: Phase of Passive Resistance (1906-1914)
        - Introduction to Satyagraha
            - Starting in 1906, this phase marked Gandhi's use of passive resistance or civil disobedience, which he named Satyagraha—devotion to truth through non-violent resistance.
        - Satyagraha Against Registration Certificates (1906)
            - The Asiatic Registration Bill required Indians to carry registration certificates with fingerprints, a discriminatory measure. Gandhi formed the Passive Resistance Association to defy this law, encouraging Indians to face penalties rather than comply.
            - He and others were jailed for refusing registration. When authorities tricked some into registering, Gandhi led a public burning of the certificates. A compromise eventually ended the standoff.
            - During this campaign, Gandhi abandoned his legal practice to focus fully on the struggle.
        - Campaign Against Restriction on Indian Migration
            - The movement expanded to oppose new laws restricting Indian migration between provinces. Indians defied these by crossing borders and refusing to show licenses, leading to many arrests.
        - Campaign Against Poll Tax
            - A £3 poll tax burdened ex-indentured Indians earning less than 10 shillings monthly. Gandhi's campaign to abolish it broadened the movement's support base.
        - Campaign Against Invalidation of Indian Marriages
            - A Supreme Court ruling invalidated non-Christian marriages, affecting Hindu, Muslim, and Parsi unions and deeming their children illegitimate. This insulted Indian women, drawing many into the movement.
        - Tolstoy Farm (1910)
            - As Satyagrahis courted arrests, funds to support their families dwindled. Gandhi established Tolstoy Farm, funded by Hermann Kallenbach (a German architect friend), Jamshedji Tata, the Nizam of Hyderabad, the INC, and the Muslim League, to house these families.
        - Indian Relief Act of 1914
            - Through negotiations with Lord Hardinge, C.F. Andrews, and General Smuts, an agreement was reached. The South African government conceded key demands: abolishing the poll tax, easing registration certificate rules, recognizing Indian marriages, and promising sympathetic treatment of immigration issues. 

    - Gandhi in India (January 9, 1915)
        - Feeling his South African mission was complete, Gandhi planned to return to India. Gokhale, his political mentor, urged him to stop in London first. Arriving during the First World War, Gandhi organized an Ambulance Corps to aid the British, earning the Kaiser-e-Hind Gold Medal in 1915.
        - He arrived in India on January 9, 1915, at age 46. Gokhale wanted him to join the Servants of India Society, but opposition from some members prevented this.
        - Gandhi spent his first year traveling India, studying its issues, and establishing the Satyagraha Ashram in Ahmedabad (later moved to the Sabarmati River banks, becoming Sabarmati Ashram).
        - Annie Besant invited him to join her Home Rule League Movement, but he declined, not wanting to trouble the British during the war.
        - At the 1916 Lucknow INC session, Gandhi remained neutral between Moderates and Extremists, successfully passing a resolution to end indentured labor recruitment.

    - Early Experiments in India
        - Champaran Satyagraha (1917) - First Civil Disobedience Movement
            - Issue
                - European planters forced Champaran (Bihar) peasants to grow indigo on 3/20 of their land (tinkathia system). When German synthetic dyes replaced indigo, planters demanded high rents and illegal dues to maximize profits, forcing peasants to sell at fixed low prices.
            - Action
                - Invited by local farmer Rajkumar Shukla, Gandhi investigated despite initial ignorance of the issue (noted at the 1916 Lucknow session). Authorities ordered him to leave, but he defied them with passive resistance, a novel tactic then.
                - Permitted to inquire, Gandhi joined the Champaran Agrarian Committee, convincing authorities to abolish the tinkathia system and compensate peasants (25% of illegal dues, per the Champaran Agrarian Act of 1917).
            - Support
                - Leaders like Rajendra Prasad, Mazharul-Haq, Mahadeo Desai, Narhari Parekh, J.B. Kripalani, Brajkishore Prasad, Anugrah Narayan Sinha, Ramnavmi Prasad, and Shambhusharan Varma assisted.
        - Kheda Satyagraha (1918) - First Non-Cooperation Movement
            - Issue
                - A 1918 drought in Kheda, Gujarat, caused crop failure. The Revenue Code entitled farmers to tax remission if yields fell below one-fourth normal, but the government refused despite Gujarat Sabha petitions (Gandhi as president).
            - Action
                - Gandhi urged farmers to withhold taxes. Sardar Vallabhbhai Patel, Narahari Parikh, Mohanlal Pandya, and Ravi Shankar Vyas organized villagers, maintaining discipline across communities.
                - When property was seized, buyers were ostracized. Over 2,000 peasants pledged non-payment, but a good Rabi crop and government concessions (tax only from those able to pay) led Gandhi to end the agitation, despite limited participation (70 of 559 villages).
            - Impact
                - Though a token victory, it expanded Gandhi's rural Gujarat base.
        - Ahmedabad Mill Strike (1918) - First Hunger Strike
            - Issue
                - A dispute arose between Ahmedabad cotton mill owners and workers over ending a plague bonus. Wartime inflation doubled prices, prompting workers to demand a 50% wage hike; owners offered 25%. Workers struck, facing arbitrary dismissals.
            - Action
                - Anusuya Sarabhai, sister of mill owner Ambalal Sarabhai, sought Gandhi's mediation. He advised a 35% demand and non-violent strike. When talks stalled, Gandhi fasted unto death—his first—bolstering workers and pressuring owners. A tribunal awarded a 35% hike, ending the strike.
        - Rowlatt Satyagraha (1919)
            - Background
                - In 1917, the Rowlatt Committee (chaired by Justice Sydney Rowlatt) investigated “revolutionary crime,” recommending repressive laws alongside the Montagu-Chelmsford report. The Rowlatt Act (March 21, 1919) continued wartime restrictions, allowed trials by special courts (no appeals, in-camera evidence), warrantless arrests, and detention up to two years.
                    - Rowlatt Act (1919)
                        - The Rowlatt Act, officially known as the Anarchical and Revolutionary Crimes Act, 1919, was a draconian law passed by the British government to curb revolutionary activities in India.
                        - Provisions of the Act:
                            - Allowed arrests without trial and detention without evidence.
                            - Restricted freedom of speech and press.
                            - Enabled the government to detain political activists indefinitely.
                            - Empowered British officials to conduct secret trials without juries.
            - Gandhi's Response
                - On February 24, 1919, Gandhi formed the Satyagraha Sabha in Bombay to resist the Act, with members pledging defiance. He said, “We shall obtain salvation only through suffering, not by reforms from the English—they use brute force, we soul force.”
                    - Phrase analysis
                        - "We shall obtain salvation only through suffering"
                            - This suggests that freedom and justice require enduring hardships and struggles rather than expecting favors from rulers.
                        - "Not by reforms from the English"
                            - It rejects the idea that British-led reforms (such as constitutional changes or legal amendments) can bring real independence.
                        - "They use brute force, we soul force"
                            - This contrasts British military power (brute force) with the moral and spiritual strength of Indians (soul force), which became the foundation of nonviolent resistance movements.
                - He called for a nationwide hartal on April 6, 1919 (initially March 30), involving work stoppage, fasting, and prayer, and sold banned books like *Hind Swaraj* as defiance.
                - Arrested on April 8 en route to Punjab, his detention sparked violence, including the Jallianwala Bagh Massacre (April 13, 1919) in Amritsar under General Dyer. Gandhi halted the Satyagraha on April 18, calling it a “Himalayan Blunder” due to unprepared participants.
                    - Jallianwala Bagh Massacre (April 13, 1919)
                        - The Jallianwala Bagh Massacre was one of the most brutal events in Indian history, where British forces under General Reginald Dyer fired on a peaceful gathering, killing hundreds of innocent Indians.
                        - Background:
                            - The British government passed the Rowlatt Act (1919), allowing arrests without trial, leading to widespread protests.
                            - On April 10, 1919, two nationalist leaders, Dr. Saifuddin Kitchlew and Dr. Satyapal, were arrested in Amritsar, sparking further outrage.
                        - The Massacre:
                            - Date: April 13, 1919 (Baisakhi - a major Sikh festival).
                            - Location: Jallianwala Bagh, Amritsar (a walled garden with only one narrow exit).
                            - Event: Thousands of men, women, and children gathered to protest peacefully.
                            - Action: General Reginald Dyer, without warning, ordered firing on the crowd, blocking the only exit.
                        - Casualties:
                            - Official British figures: ~379 killed, 1,200 wounded.
                            - Indian estimates: Over 1,000 deaths.
                        - Aftermath:
                            - Dyer justified his actions, calling it a necessary lesson to "teach Indians obedience."
                            - Rabindranath Tagore renounced his knighthood in protest.
                            - Mahatma Gandhi intensified the Non-Cooperation Movement (1920).
                        - Impact:
                            - Exposed British brutality and shattered Indian trust in British rule.
                            - Strengthened Indian nationalism and the demand for complete independence.
                            - Led to the formation of the Hunter Commission, which condemned Dyer, but he was still honored by the British in England.
                                - Hunter Commission (1919-1920)
                                    - After the Jallianwala Bagh Massacre, the British government appointed the Hunter Commission in October 1919 to investigate the incident.
                                    - Composition of the Commission:
                                        - Led by Lord William Hunter.
                                        - Included British and Indian members but had a British majority.
                                    - Findings and Report:
                                        - Condemned General Dyer for his actions but did not hold him criminally accountable.
                                        - Acknowledged that the firing was excessive but did not suggest strict punishment for Dyer.
                                        - Dyer was forced to retire, but in Britain, he was hailed as a hero.
                                    - Indian Reaction:
                                        - Indians rejected the commission's findings as biased and unjust.
                                        - Rabindranath Tagore renounced his knighthood in protest.
                                        - Gandhi intensified Non-Cooperation Movement (1920).
            - Opposition
                - Liberals (Surendranath Banerjee, D.E. Wacha, T.B. Sapru, Srinivas Shastri), Annie Besant (no civil resistance warranted), and Tilak's followers (N.C. Kelkar, G.S. Khaparde) opposed it, fearing it would derail constitutional reforms.
            - Support
                - Young Home Rule League members and Pan-Islamic leaders (Abdul Bari, Dr. M.A. Ansari) backed Gandhi, spurred by the Khilafat issue post-World War I.

    - Gandhian Ideology  
        - Influences  
            - In My Experiments with Truth, Gandhi credits his parents, his family's socio-religious milieu (environment), the Bhagavad Gita, Jainism, and later, thinkers like Tolstoy, Thoreau, and Ruskin for shaping his philosophy.  
            - Leo Tolstoy's "The Kingdom of God is Within You" reinforced his belief in non-violence.  
            - Henry David Thoreau's "Civil Disobedience" influenced his concept of passive resistance.  
            - John Ruskin's "Unto This Last" shaped his ideas on economic decentralization and dignity of labor.  
        - Fundamental Principles  
            - Satya (Truth) - The foundation of Gandhi's thought; he saw truth as inseparable from morality and God.  
            - Ahimsa (Non-Violence) - Inspired by Jainism and the Bhagavad Gita, he believed in non-violent resistance against oppression.  
            - Asteya (Non-Stealing) - Extending beyond theft, he saw it as refraining from exploitation of any kind.  
            - Aparigraha (Non-Possession) - Advocated minimalism, rejecting material excess.  
            - Brahmacharya (Self-Control) - Initially referring to celibacy, he later expanded it to self-discipline in all aspects of life.  
        - Tools  
            - Swaraj (Self-rule) - More than political independence, he viewed it as moral and spiritual self-governance.  
            - Swadeshi (Self-reliance) - Promotion of indigenous industries, particularly Khadi, to counter economic dependence on Britain.  
            - Sarvodaya (Upliftment of all) - Based on egalitarian principles, advocating welfare for all sections of society.  
        - Swaraj  
            - In Hind Swaraj (1909), Gandhi saw modern Western civilization, not just British rule, as the real enemy. He criticized industrialization for exploiting rural India, arguing that railways and mechanization facilitated famines by enabling food exports while Indians starved.  
            - Critics called him obscurantist (opposing progress), but he emphasized modernization's human cost. His vision of Swaraj evolved from a simple, traditional Indian lifestyle to village reconstruction, Khadi promotion, and Harijan welfare.  
        - Swadeshi  
            - He rejected Western cultural domination but encouraged Indian industries. Despite his opposition to capitalism, he collaborated with industrialists like Ambalal Sarabhai and G.D. Birla, believing in the Trusteeship theory - where capitalists were stewards of wealth meant for society's welfare.  
        - Sarvodaya  
            - Meaning “upliftment of all,” it sought universal welfare through voluntary effort. Gandhi's ideal society, Ram Rajya, was a cooperative, stateless entity thriving on self-sufficiency.  
            - Echoing Thoreau's philosophy (“the best government governs least”), he envisioned villages self-regulating through moral principles rather than legal enforcement.  
        - Religion  
            - He believed religion should permeate (spread or diffuse through something completely) all actions, not remain confined to private life. His universalism allowed him to appreciate all faiths while remaining deeply rooted in his own.  
            - “I want all cultures to blow about my house freely, but I refuse to be blown off my feet.”  
                - This quote by Gandhi means that he was open to learning from and appreciating all cultures, allowing their ideas and values to influence him. However, he would not let them shake or uproot his own core beliefs and identity.
        - Economic Decentralization  
            - Advocated small-scale, community-driven industries over large-scale industrialization to prevent economic disparity and alienation.  
        - Varna System and Non-Possession  
            - He interpreted varna (caste-based division of labor) as a system of duty rather than privilege, though this stance was widely criticized.  
        - Education  
            - The 1937 Wardha Scheme (published in Harijan) proposed "Nai Talim" (Basic Education), advocating skill-based learning integrated with moral values.  
        - Women  
            - He opposed all forms of gender discrimination, advocating perfect equality in both personal and public spheres.  
        - Untouchability  
            - He called it a “crime against humanity” and devoted a significant part of his activism to its eradication, renaming Dalits as "Harijans" (Children of God).              
                        

- Indian National Movement (1917-1934) - Gandhian Phase
    - Non-Cooperation and Khilafat Movement
        - Overview
            - The Khilafat Movement and Non-Cooperation Movement, though distinct in their origins and aims, converged in the early 1920s into a unified mass campaign under Mahatma Gandhi's leadership, marking a pivotal moment in India's struggle against British rule.
            - The Khilafat Movement stemmed from Muslim outrage over the dismantling of the Ottoman Caliphate post-World War I, while the Non-Cooperation Movement sought broader Indian self-governance, leveraging widespread discontent with colonial policies.
            - Their alliance fostered unprecedented Hindu-Muslim unity, amplifying resistance until its abrupt halt in 1922.
        - The Khilafat Movement
            - Background
                - Muslims globally, including India's 70 million-strong community, revered the Sultan of Turkey as the Khalifa, their spiritual leader. During World War I (1914–1918), Turkey allied with the Axis powers against Britain, earning Indian Muslim sympathy as a religious cause.
                - Post-war, the Treaty of Sèvres (August 1920) dismembered the Ottoman Empire, stripped the Khalifa of authority, and redistributed territories, igniting global Muslim fury and fueling agitation in India.
            - Muslim Demands from the British
                - Retention of the Khalifa's control over Islamic holy sites (e.g., Mecca, Medina) to preserve religious authority.
                - Assurance of sufficient territories for the Khalifa in post-war arrangements, opposing the severe territorial losses imposed by the Allies.
            - Formation of the Khilafat Committee
                - In early 1919, leaders like Shaukat Ali and Muhammad Ali (the Ali brothers), Maulana Abul Kalam Azad, Hakim Ajmal Khan, and Hasrat Mohani formed the Khilafat Committee to lobby Britain for a reversal of its Turkey policy.
                - Initially, it employed moderate tactics—public meetings, petitions, and delegations—to advocate for the Khalifa's reinstatement.
                - By late 1919, a militant faction emerged, pushing for active resistance, including non-cooperation with British authorities, aligning with growing anti-colonial sentiment.
            - All India Khilafat Conference (November 22–23, 1919)
                - Held in Delhi, this conference saw militant leaders escalate their call for non-cooperation with the British government in India to force concessions on Turkey.
                - In April 1920, Shaukat Ali threatened a joint Hindu-Muslim non-cooperation campaign under Gandhi if demands went unmet, leading to Gandhi's appointment as president of the All-India Khilafat Committee.
            - Early Actions
                - Early 1920: A joint Hindu-Muslim deputation met Viceroy Lord Chelmsford to press Khilafat grievances, but achieved little, intensifying agitation.
        - The Non-Cooperation Movement
            - Background
                - Sparked by mounting frustration with British rule, the movement was catalyzed by the Jallianwala Bagh massacre (April 1919) and the inadequate Government of India Act 1919, which offered limited self-governance under continued British dominance.
                - Gandhi envisioned it as a non-violent mass campaign to achieve Swaraj (self-rule), capitalizing on national unrest beyond the Khilafat issue.
            - Objectives
                - Redress of Punjab wrongs, notably the Jallianwala Bagh atrocities and martial law excesses of 1919.
                - Attainment of Swaraj through peaceful, extra-constitutional means, shifting from Congress's earlier goal of gradual self-government within the empire.
            - Initial Steps
                - February 1920: Gandhi declared the Khilafat issue, alongside Punjab grievances and stalled reforms, as justification for non-cooperation, threatening action if the peace treaty dissatisfied Indians.
                - June 1920: An all-party conference in Allahabad approved boycotts of schools, colleges, and law courts, appointing Gandhi to lead, marking a shift to mass resistance.
            - Indian National Congress (INC) Dynamics
                - Gandhi favored Satyagraha and non-cooperation, but the INC was split: some opposed his broad program, especially the council boycott, reflecting tensions between radicals and moderates like Annie Besant and Madan Mohan Malaviya.
                - Bal Gangadhar Tilak resisted a Hindu-Muslim alliance, advocating a secular nationalist approach, a stance shared by much of the INC's old guard.
        - Convergence of Khilafat and Non-Cooperation Movements
            - Muslim League and Congress Alliance
                - The Muslim League pledged full support to Congress-led agitations, forging a Hindu-Muslim partnership despite Tilak's secular objections and resistance from INC conservatives.
                - Gandhi bridged the movements, merging Khilafat's religious fervor with Non-Cooperation's nationalist goals.
            - Launch of Joint Campaign (August 1920)
                - August 31, 1920: Tilak's death on August 1—a major blow to moderates—preceded the Khilafat Committee's formal non-cooperation launch in September, aligning with Gandhi's broader strategy.
            - Calcutta Special Session (September 1920)
                - Presided by Lala Lajpat Rai, this INC session endorsed non-cooperation until Punjab and Khilafat wrongs were rectified and Swaraj achieved.
                - Despite widespread support, opposition from C.R. Das, Madan Mohan Malaviya, and Annie Besant highlighted internal divisions over Gandhi's radical tactics.
            - Nagpur Session (December 1920)
                - Presided by C. Vijayaraghavachariar, this session solidified non-cooperation for Khilafat redress, Punjab justice, and Swaraj, redefining Congress's aim to Swaraj via peaceful, legitimate mass struggle.
                - Organizational Changes (New Constitution by Gandhi)
                    - Congress Committees established from village to provincial levels for grassroots mobilization.
                    - An All-India Committee of 350 members, with a 15-member executive, enabled year-round operations, fulfilling Tilak's 1916 demand.
                    - Provincial committees reorganized on linguistic lines for regional coherence.
                    - Hindi promoted as the language for Congress work, enhancing accessibility.
                    - Membership opened to all over 21 for a four-anna annual fee, democratizing participation.
                - Gandhi's Vision
                    - Defined Swaraj as self-government within the empire if feasible, or independence if necessary, predicting its realization within a year with full commitment.
                - Dissenters' Exodus
                    - Leaders like Muhammad Ali Jinnah, Annie Besant, G.S. Khaparde, and Bipin Chandra Pal left Congress for constitutional methods; Surendranath Banerjee founded the Indian National Liberal Federation as an alternative.
        - Phases of the Joint Non-Cooperation Khilafat Movement
            - Program of Non-Cooperation
                - Destructive Steps
                    - Boycott of government schools and colleges, promoting national alternatives.
                    - Boycott of law courts, with justice dispensed through local panchayats.
                    - Boycott of foreign cloth, encouraging khadi and hand-spinning as symbols of self-reliance.
                    - Renunciation of government honors and titles, rejecting British legitimacy.
                    - Boycott of legislative councils, with massive participation—Congress candidates withdrew, and voters abstained, though some leaders dissented.
                - Constructive Steps
                    - Establishment of national institutions and promotion of Hindu-Muslim unity to build a self-governing framework.
            - First Phase (January to March 1921)
                - Gandhi and the Ali brothers toured India, inspiring thousands of students to abandon government institutions for about 800 national schools and colleges, led by Acharya Narendra Dev, C.R. Das, Lala Lajpat Rai, Zakir Hussain, and Subhash Chandra Bose (principal of National College, Calcutta). 
                    - Key institutions included Jamia Millia Islamia, Kashi Vidyapeeth, Gujarat Vidyapeeth, and Bihar Vidyapeeth.
                - Prominent lawyers—Motilal Nehru, Jawaharlal Nehru, C.R. Das, C. Rajagopalachari, Saifuddin Kitchlew, Vallabhbhai Patel, Asaf Ali, T. Prakasam, and Rajendra Prasad—quit their practices, embracing the boycott.
                - Foreign cloth imports halved as public bonfires consumed imports; picketing (a form of protest in which individuals assemble outside an establishment to dissuade others from entering or conducting business with it) targeted foreign liquor and toddy shops to promote temperance (refers to the movement aimed at reducing or eliminating the consumption of alcohol).
            - Second Phase (April to June 1921)
                - Focused on raising one crore rupees for the Tilak Swaraj Fund by August 1921, enrolling one crore Congress members, and installing 20 lakh charkhas by June 1930 (an extended target). The Fund exceeded its goal, collecting one crore rupees, reflecting robust public support.
            - Third Phase (July to November 1921)
                - Emphasized boycotting foreign cloth, opposing the Prince of Wales' visit (November 17, 1921), and promoting charkhas and khadi, with Gandhi—nicknamed a “half-naked fakir” by Winston Churchill—leading symbolically.
                - Congress volunteer corps (a group of people organized for a specific purpose, usually within a military, professional, or specialized context) acted as a parallel police force; local bodies initiated civil disobedience where ready, sparking no-tax movements in Midnapore (Bengal) against union board taxes and Guntur (Andhra) against municipal levies.
                - In Assam, strikes disrupted tea plantations, steamer services, and the Assam-Bengal Railways, signaling widespread defiance.
                - The Prince of Wales' arrival triggered nationwide strikes and protests; Gandhi lit a massive bonfire of foreign cloth at Elphinstone Mill, Bombay, owned by nationalist Umar Shobhani, amplifying Swadeshi fervor.
                - Local movements—Awadh Kisan Movement (UP), Eka Movement (UP), Mappila Revolt (Malabar), and Sikh agitation against mahants in Punjab—flourished, blending regional grievances with national goals.
        - Government Response
            - May 1921: The Gandhi-Reading Talks saw Viceroy Lord Reading urge Gandhi to restrain the Ali brothers' inflammatory speeches, aiming to divide leaders, but the effort failed as unity held.
            - Late 1921: Repression intensified—Congress and Khilafat volunteer corps were outlawed, public gatherings banned, newspapers censored, offices raided at midnight, police deployed lathis and gunfire against Satyagrahis, and by December, most leaders except Gandhi were jailed.
        - INC vs. Government
            - Ahmedabad Session (December 1921)
                - Presided by C.R. Das (jailed, with Hakim Ajmal Khan), this session, amid growing grassroots pressure, made Gandhi sole authority on launching civil disobedience.
                - Hasrat Mohani demanded Poorna Swaraj (complete independence) and coined "Inqalab Zindabad" (Long Live Revolution), a radical call Gandhi opposed, favoring phased self-rule.
            - Bardoli Ultimatum
                - February 1, 1922: Gandhi threatened civil disobedience from Bardoli, Gujarat, starting February 5 unless political prisoners were freed and press curbs lifted, a plan derailed by Chauri Chaura.
        - Chauri Chaura Incident
            - Event (February 5, 1922)
                - In Chauri Chaura (UP), the police brutally assaulted a volunteer leader who was campaigning against liquor sales and high food prices. In retaliation, an enraged crowd gathered in protest. The police opened fire on the demonstrators, escalating tensions further. In response, the mob set fire to the police station, killing 22 officers. Those attempting to escape were caught, attacked, and thrown back into the flames.
            - Congress Response
                - February 1922: The Congress Working Committee convened in Bardoli and decided to halt all law-breaking activities. Instead, the movement was redirected toward constructive programs such as the promotion of khadi, establishment of national schools, advocacy for temperance, Hindu-Muslim unity, and campaigns against untouchability.
            - Internal Dissent
                - Prominent leaders, including C.R. Das, Motilal Nehru, Subhash Chandra Bose, and Jawaharlal Nehru, strongly opposed Gandhi's decision to suspend the movement. They argued that calling off the agitation over a localized incident weakened national momentum and demoralized activists.
            - Gandhi's Arrest
                - On March 10, 1922, Gandhi was arrested and charged with sedition. He was sentenced to six years of imprisonment at Yerwada Jail, Poona. However, due to deteriorating health and an appendicitis surgery, he was released early on February 5, 1924.            
        - Participation of Various Sections
            - Middle Class
                - In elite centers like Calcutta, Bombay, and Madras, enthusiasm was tepid (lukewarm or unenthusiastic), with few resigning government posts or titles. Newcomers like Rajendra Prasad (Bihar) and Vallabhbhai Patel (Gujarat) emerged as key voices.
            - Business Class
                - Supported economic boycotts, benefiting from Swadeshi, though some industrialists hesitated, fearing labor unrest—a concern later validated by strikes in Bombay and Ahmedabad.
            - Peasants
                - Channeled frustrations against landlords and traders, often exceeding Gandhi's non-violent framework to target both British rule and local oppressors.
            - Students
                - Thousands abandoned government schools and colleges, becoming vital volunteers, infusing the movement with youthful energy.
            - Women
                - Participated in unprecedented numbers, picketing shops and marching, significantly bolstering (strengthening, supporting, or reinforcing) the campaign.
            - Hindu-Muslim Unity
                - Achieved remarkable solidarity (unity or agreement of feeling or action), a major success despite communal flare-ups like the Mappila Uprisings in Malabar, where violence marred (damaged, spoiled, or impaired) the alliance.
                    - Mappila Uprisings (1836–1921)
                        - Background
                            - Mappilas were a Muslim community in Malabar, consisting of peasants, traders, and fishermen.
                            - The oppressive agrarian system under British rule heavily favored Hindu landlords (jenmis), leading to severe exploitation of Mappila tenants.
                            - Over time, economic distress and religious grievances fueled multiple uprisings.
                        - Key Uprisings Before 1921
                            - Early Revolts (1836–1919)
                                - Uprisings began in the early 19th century as small, localized acts of resistance against landlords and British authorities.
                                - The British crushed these uprisings with extreme force, using martial law and mass executions.
                            - Mappila Rebellion (1921)
                                - Part of the larger Khilafat and Non-Cooperation Movement, this revolt was more organized and widespread.
                                - Mappila peasants, influenced by Khilafat leaders and anti-British sentiment, attacked British officials and landlords.
                                - The British brutally suppressed the rebellion, killing thousands and deporting many rebels to the Andaman Islands.
                        - Consequences
                            - The rebellion led to harsher repression by the British, including mass arrests, executions, and the infamous Wagon Tragedy, where 64 Mappila prisoners suffocated to death in a closed railway wagon.
                            - The revolt created tensions between Hindus and Muslims, as some Hindu landlords were also targeted.
                            - The Congress and Khilafat leaders distanced themselves from the rebellion, calling it misdirected violence.
        - Aftermath and Transition  
            - Why Withdrawal? (Gandhi's Rationale)  
                - Gandhi believed the masses had not fully internalized non-violence, and the Chauri Chaura incident gave Britain an opportunity to suppress the movement with harsher repression.  
                - He feared that continued agitation could escalate into widespread violence, which would not only undermine the movement's credibility but also justify British retaliation.  
                - Gandhi viewed Satyagraha as a moral force rather than just a political tool. The turn toward violence, in his view, compromised the ethical foundation of the movement.  
            - Other Causes for Decline  
                - By late 1921, exhaustion and disillusionment had set in among the masses. Repetitive boycotts and protests were proving unsustainable, especially as they did not yield immediate political gains.  
                - Khilafat Movement's Decline:  
                    - The fall of the Caliphate following Mustafa Kemal Pasha's reforms in Turkey (abolishing the Sultanate in 1922 and the Caliphate in 1924) weakened Muslim support for the Non-Cooperation Movement.  
                    - With its religious anchor gone, Hindu-Muslim unity, which had been a key driving force, began to weaken.  
            - Congress's Reaction and Internal Dissent  
                - Gandhi's unilateral decision to call off the movement stunned and divided the Congress leadership.  
                - Leaders such as Motilal Nehru, C.R. Das, and Subhash Chandra Bose strongly opposed the decision:  
                    - Subhash Bose, in his book *The Indian Struggle*, called the withdrawal a national calamity, arguing that it weakened India's resistance against British rule.  
                    - He questioned why the entire nation should suffer for the actions of a small group of villagers.  
                    - Bose believed that all mass movements carried risks, and occasional incidents of violence should not justify abandoning a nationwide struggle.  
            - End of the Movement  
                - The Civil Disobedience Enquiry Committee, set up by the Congress, concluded that India was not yet ready for large-scale civil disobedience. This reinforced the view that the movement had to be called off.  
                - Bardoli Resolution (February 12, 1922):  
                    - Congress officially condemned the Chauri Chaura violence and called for an end to non-cooperation.  
                    - The resolution urged peasants to resume tax and rent payments, marking a strategic retreat rather than an outright failure.  
                    - The movement shifted focus towards constructive programs such as khadi promotion, Hindu-Muslim unity, and anti-untouchability efforts, keeping the spirit of nationalism alive.  
            - Impact of the Withdrawal  
                - The movement's abrupt end disheartened the masses, leading to political demobilization for a few years. Many people lost faith in Gandhi's methods, believing that non-violence alone could not secure independence.  
                - It created divisions within the Congress, with many leaders favoring a more aggressive approach rather than passive resistance. This eventually led to the rise of the Swarajists, who sought to engage with the British government from within legislative councils.  
                - Despite its premature end, the movement succeeded in mobilizing Indians on an unprecedented scale, proving that mass resistance could challenge British rule.  
                - The British government, realizing the power of mass movements, grew more cautious and introduced repressive laws to prevent future uprisings.                          

    - Post Non-Cooperation Era: Swarajists & No Changers
        - Overview
            - The abrupt end of the Non-Cooperation Movement in February 1922 fractured Congress into two factions: Swarajists (Pro-Changers), who sought to enter legislative councils to obstruct British rule from within, and No-Changers, who favored continuing Gandhi's original non-violent, constructive program outside councils.
        - Formation of Swaraj Party (January 1, 1923)
            - Context
                - Gandhi's suspension caused widespread disappointment and a leadership rift. The All India Congress Committee (AICC) formed the Civil Disobedience Inquiry Committee in 1922, advising President C.R. Das that India wasn't ready to relaunch Non-Cooperation. It recommended a mass movement rooted in non-violence, educating farmers and laborers, and promoting khadi, village enterprises, and Hindu-Muslim unity.
                - Post-1922, communal tensions flared—Multan (September 1922), Amritsar (April 1923), and Calcutta (May 1923)—complicating unity efforts.
            - Gaya Congress Session (December 1922)
                - Presided by C.R. Das, this session saw his council-entry proposal defeated by No-Changers. Das resigned as president, and Motilal Nehru stepped down as General Secretary, though both remained in Congress.
            - Establishment
                - On January 1, 1923, C.R. Das, with Motilal Nehru as secretary, founded the Congress-Khilafat Swaraj Party (Swaraj Party). Leaders included N.C. Kelkar, Huseyn Shaheed Suhrawardy, Subhash Chandra Bose, and Vithalbhai Patel.
                - The deadlock between equally dominant factions led to a compromise to avoid a repeat of the 1907 Surat Split.
        - 
        - A Group Within the Congress
            - Strategy
                - Following Gandhi's advice, Swarajists stayed within Congress but operated independently to maintain unity.
            - Congress Special Session (Delhi, September 1923)
                - Presided by Maulana Azad, this session permitted Congressmen to contest elections, balancing Swarajist ambitions with No-Changer principles.
            - Kakinada Session (December 1923)
                - Allowed council entry, positing (to put forward an idea, argument, or assumption) non-cooperation could be practiced within councils, averting (preventing or avoiding something, especially a negative event or consequence) a formal split.
        - Swarajists in Action
            - First Gathering (Allahabad, 1923)
                - Declared ‘Dominion Status' as an interim goal, leaving long-term aims vague, reflecting strategic flexibility.
            - Election Manifesto
                - Emphasized Indian control over governance, disrupting the 1919 reforms, halting administrative functions, aligning policies with Indian perspectives, and supporting Gandhi's constructive initiatives outside councils.
            - Evolution
                - The party's constitution and ideology solidified at the 1924 Belgaum Session, presided by Gandhi, aiming for Swaraj through lawful, non-violent means, though skeptical of mass political involvement.
        - Party Makes Its Mark
            - 
            - General Elections (November 1923)
                - Contested the Central Legislative Assembly (145 seats, 105 elected) and Provincial Assemblies.
                - Allied with M.A. Jinnah to form the Nationalist Party in the Central Assembly, with Motilal Nehru as opposition leader, dubbed the ‘Parliamentary Wing of the Congress.'
                - In March 1925, elected Vithalbhai Patel as President (Speaker) of the Central Legislative Assembly, a historic first for an Indian.
                - Emerged as the largest party in Bombay, Central Assembly, and Bengal Councils, forming alliances to challenge the government consistently.
                - 
        - Swarajists Mean Business
            - Obstructionist Strategy
                - Focused on blocking reforms internally, a tactic British authorities found harder to counter than outright rebellion. Rejected votable budget parts, vetoed bureaucratic legislation, and affirmed Indian rights.
                - C.R. Das declared: “I want you to enter the Councils, secure a majority, and put forward national demands. If rejected, oppose the Government in every measure, good, bad, or indifferent, and make Council work impossible.”
            - Constructive Aims
                - Established economic policies to curb wealth outflow, boosted local industries, and championed workers' rights, aligning with Swadeshi ideals.
        - Gandhi's Evolving Stance
            - Initial Opposition
                - Released from jail in February 1924, Gandhi ideologically opposed council entry, clashing with Swarajists.
            - Ahmedabad AICC Meeting (June 1924)
                - Pushed the Hand Spinning Resolution, mandating dissenters to resign from AICC, aiming to sideline Swarajists, but their growing strength forced a rethink.
            - Compromise
                - Gandhi moderated his stance, eventually endorsing Swarajists as Congress's official negotiators with the government.
            - Belgaum Session (December 1924)
                - The only session Gandhi presided over, it established a truce: Swarajists gained a Working Committee majority, while constructive programs remained the primary Swaraj strategy.
        - Achievements of Swarajists
            - Legislative Achievements
                - Advocated for political prisoners' release, provincial autonomy, and an immediate Round Table Conference for self-governance.
                - Defeated the Public Safety Bill (1928), which targeted socialists and communists with detention without trial.
                    - Public Safety Bill (1928)
                        - Background
                            - The Public Safety Bill (1928) was introduced by the British government in response to growing revolutionary activities in India, particularly the increasing influence of Bhagat Singh, Hindustan Republican Association (HRA), and Communist movements. 
                            - The British feared that socialist and revolutionary ideas were spreading rapidly, especially among workers and peasants.
                        - Provisions of the Bill
                            - Allowed the government to detain and deport individuals suspected of engaging in anti-government activities, particularly communists and revolutionaries.
                            - Targeted trade unions and labor movements, fearing they were influenced by communist ideology.
                            - Provided sweeping powers to suppress political dissent without fair trials.
                        - Opposition and Rejection
                            - The bill was strongly opposed by nationalists, especially Motilal Nehru and Jinnah, who viewed it as an attack on civil liberties.
                            - Jinnah's famous speech: He criticized the bill, stating it would "establish a lawless state under the pretext of safeguarding law and order."
                            - Due to massive opposition, the bill was defeated in the Legislative Assembly.
                        - Aftermath and Impact
                            - Despite its failure in the Assembly, the British government later enforced similar provisions under the Trade Disputes Act of 1929 and Public Safety Ordinances.
                            - The bill's rejection symbolized growing Indian unity against repressive colonial laws.
                            - It fueled further radicalization, leading revolutionaries like Bhagat Singh to adopt more aggressive methods against British rule.
                - Overthrew dyarchy in Bengal and Central Provinces, cut salt tax and train fares, secured tax relief, scrapped excise duties, and pushed tariffs for local industries.
                - Passed key laws: Workmen's Compensation Act (1923), Indian Trade Union Act (1926), Indian Bar Councils Act (1926), and Child Marriage Prevention Act (1926).
                    - Workmen's Compensation Act (1923)
                        - Objective: Provided financial compensation to workers injured or killed in industrial accidents.
                        - Key Provisions:
                            - Employers were mandated to compensate workers for injuries or death caused during employment.
                            - Compensation was based on the severity of the injury and the worker's wage.
                            - Exempted cases where workers were at fault due to negligence or intoxication.
                        - Impact:
                            - Marked the first step toward labor welfare legislation in India.
                            - Reduced financial burden on injured workers and their families.
                            - Laid the foundation for future labor laws and social security measures.
                    - Indian Trade Union Act (1926)
                        - Objective: Legalized trade unions and provided them with protections.
                        - Key Provisions:
                            - Recognized trade unions as legal entities, allowing them to represent workers.
                            - Gave unions immunity from prosecution for legitimate strike activities.
                            - Required unions to register with the government and follow certain rules.
                        - Impact:
                            - Strengthened the labor movement by giving unions legal standing.
                            - Encouraged workers to organize and demand better wages and working conditions.
                            - Set the stage for future labor rights and union protections.
                    - Indian Bar Councils Act (1926)
                        - Objective: Regulated the legal profession and established autonomous bar councils.
                        - Key Provisions:
                            - Created Bar Councils in each High Court jurisdiction to regulate lawyers.
                            - Gave councils the power to discipline lawyers and uphold ethical standards.
                            - Ensured representation of lawyers in legal affairs and professional governance.
                        - Impact:
                            - Enhanced self-regulation of the legal profession.
                            - Strengthened lawyers' autonomy and their role in nationalist politics.
                            - Became the foundation for the Bar Council of India, formed after independence.
                    - Child Marriage Prevention Act (1929) (Sarda Act)
                        - Objective: Raised the legal marriage age to curb child marriage.
                        - Key Provisions:
                            - Increased the minimum marriage age to 14 for girls and 18 for boys.
                            - Penalized those involved in child marriages, including parents and priests.
                            - Empowered courts to intervene and annul child marriages.
                        - Impact:
                            - Marked the first major legal reform against child marriage in India.
                            - Faced strong opposition from orthodox communities but set a precedent for later reforms.
                            - Post-independence, the minimum age was further increased to 18 for girls and 21 for boys under the Prohibition of Child Marriage Act (2006).
                - Repealed (revoke or annul) repressive laws like the State Prisoners Act (1850) and Prevention of Seditious Meetings Act (1921).
                    - State Prisoners Act (1850)
                        - Objective: Gave the British government the power to detain individuals deemed a threat to the state.
                        - Key Provisions:
                            - Allowed the detention of any person without trial if they were considered dangerous to the colonial government.
                            - The government could imprison political dissidents, revolutionaries, or individuals suspected of plotting against British rule.
                            - Provided no right to appeal or legal representation for the detainees.
                        - Impact:
                            - Used to suppress political activists and revolutionaries during the 19th and early 20th centuries.
                            - Enabled the government to arbitrarily arrest nationalists without needing evidence or a trial.
                            - Laid the foundation for later preventive detention laws in colonial and post-independence India.
                    - Prevention of Seditious Meetings Act (1921)
                        - Objective: Controlled public gatherings to prevent anti-British protests and movements.
                        - Key Provisions:
                            - Gave district magistrates the power to prohibit public meetings that could incite rebellion.
                            - Required government permission for political meetings in designated areas.
                            - Allowed officials to disperse gatherings and arrest participants if they were deemed seditious.
                        - Impact:
                            - Used to curb nationalist movements, particularly after the Non-Cooperation Movement and the Rowlatt Act protests.
                            - Restricted Gandhian satyagrahas, trade union activities, and peasant uprisings.
                            - Became a tool to stifle freedom of speech and assembly, leading to growing resentment against British rule.
                - Vithalbhai Patel's election as Speaker in 1925 marked a symbolic victory.
            - Muddiman Commission
                - Swarajist pressure led to a 1924 committee investigating defects in the 1919 Act, though its recommendations were limited.
            - Filling the Political Vacuum
                - Bridged the gap left by Non-Cooperation's withdrawal, maintaining momentum until mass movements resumed.
            - Constructive Work Outside Councils
                - Promoted khadi (resisting its mandatory status for Congress membership), supported the Vaikom Satyagraha against untouchability, and advocated alcohol prohibition.
        - Failure of Swarajists
            - Challenges
                - Lacked coordination with mass struggles, limiting broader impact.
                - Obstructionist strategy reached its limits without a mass base.
                - Faced ideological conflicts with coalition partners like Jinnah's group.
                - Some succumbed to office perks, diluting their stance.
                - Failed to support peasant causes, alienating rural masses.
            - Bipin Chandra's Critique
                - “The Swarajists failed to provide a coherent strategy for the national movement, and their reliance on councils alone was a strategic error.”
        - Decline of Swaraj Party
            - Turning Points (1925 Onward)
                - C.R. Das's sudden death (June 1925) weakened leadership; communal riots eroded unity; loss of popular support and a Responsivist-Non-Responsivist split diminished cohesion.
                - Major losses in the 1926 elections signaled decline.
            - Shifting Landscape
                - The Simon Commission's arrival (1928) shifted focus to mass protests; the 1929 Lahore Congress resolution for Poorna Swaraj and the 1930 Civil Disobedience Movement prompted (caused, triggered, or encouraged an action or response) Swarajists to leave councils and merge with Congress for a unified independence push.
        - 
        - Understanding Responsivists and Non-Responsivists
            - Responsivists
                - Favored cooperation with the government and accepting office when possible; led by Madan Mohan Malaviya and Lala Lajpat Rai.
            - Non-Responsivists
                - Opposed office acceptance, prioritizing obstruction; led by Motilal Nehru.

    - March to Civil Disobedience Movement and Its Evolution
        - Overview
            - The Civil Disobedience Movement (CDM) of 1930–1934 marked a transformative escalation in India's struggle for independence, shifting from non-cooperation to direct defiance of British laws under Mahatma Gandhi's leadership. Building on the Non-Cooperation Movement's momentum (1920–1922), it aimed for Poorna Swaraj (complete independence) following disillusionment with British promises.
        - Preparatory Phase (1929–1930)
            - Gandhi's Mobilization Efforts
                - Nationwide Campaign
                    - In 1929, Gandhi toured India extensively, rallying public support for direct action. He urged youth to brace for sacrifices and emphasized village-level constructive work—khadi promotion, education, and social reform—as a foundation for resistance.
                - Boycott of Foreign Goods
                    - The Congress Working Committee (CWC) established a Foreign Cloth Boycott Committee in mid-1929, organizing public bonfires of foreign cloth and promoting Swadeshi goods to undermine British economic control, echoing the Non-Cooperation Movement's tactics.
            - Lord Irwin's Declaration (October 31, 1929) - Diwali Declaration
                - Viceroy Lord Irwin reaffirmed Dominion Status as India's ultimate constitutional goal, referencing the 1917 Montagu Declaration, and promised a Round Table Conference post-Simon Commission report. Lacking a timeline, this vague pledge disappointed Indian leaders expecting concrete steps toward self-rule.
            - Delhi Manifesto (November 2, 1929)
                - Prominent leaders—Gandhi, Jawaharlal Nehru, Motilal Nehru, and others—issued demands for participating in the Round Table Conference:
                    - It must draft a Dominion Status constitution and act as a constituent assembly.
                    - Congress should hold majority representation.
                    - General amnesty for political prisoners must be granted.
                - Irwin's rejection of these terms heightened tensions, pushing Congress toward confrontation.
            - Lahore Congress Session (December 26–31, 1929)
                - Presided by Jawaharlal Nehru, elected president at 40, symbolizing youth leadership.
                - Key Resolutions:
                    - Declared Poorna Swaraj as Congress's ultimate goal, rejecting Dominion Status.
                    - Decided to boycott the Round Table Conference, signaling distrust in British negotiations.
                    - Authorized the CWC to launch civil disobedience, including tax non-payment.
                    - Called for Congress legislators to resign, severing ties with British institutions.
                - Context: Held amid rising radicalism post-Simon Commission protests (1928) and Bhagat Singh's revolutionary acts, reflecting a shift from constitutionalism to mass action.
            - First Independence Day (January 26, 1930)
                - Designated Swarajya Day, observed nationwide on January 26, 1930, following the Lahore resolution.
                - December 31, 1929: Nehru hoisted the tricolor on the Ravi River's banks in Lahore, amid “Inquilab Zindabad” slogans, symbolizing a break from imperial allegiance.
                - Public meetings across India recited the Independence Pledge in local languages, raising the national flag in towns and villages, galvanizing mass participation.
            - Gandhi's 11-Point Ultimatum (January 31, 1930)
                - Presented to Viceroy Irwin, demands addressed diverse interests:
                    - General Interest:
                        - Reduce Army and high-grade civil service salaries by 50% to curb expenditure.
                        - Enforce total prohibition of intoxicants, targeting revenue and social ills.
                        - Reform the CID, notorious for political surveillance.
                        - Release all political prisoners, including those from Non-Cooperation and revolutionary trials.
                        - Accept the Postal Reservation Bill to reserve jobs for Indians.
                        - Amend the Arms Act of 1878 for popular control over firearm licenses, challenging British monopoly on force.
                    - Capitalists' Interests:
                        - Lower the rupee-sterling exchange rate to 1s 4d (from 1s 6d), aiding Indian exports.
                        - Reserve coastal shipping for Indians, boosting domestic trade.
                        - Protect Indian textiles against British imports, supporting industrialists.
                    - Peasants' Interests:
                        - Cut agricultural tax by 50% and place it under legislative control, easing rural burdens.
                        - Abolish the salt tax and government salt monopoly, a universal grievance.
                - Government Response:
                    - Irwin's dismissive reply offered no concessions, prompting the CWC (Congress Working Committee) to authorize Gandhi to launch the CDM at his discretion.
                    - Gandhi remarked: “On bended knees I asked for bread and received a stone instead. The English nation responds only to force.”
                        - Analysis of the statement
                            - "On bended knees I asked for bread"
                                - This signifies Gandhi's willingness to seek justice and reforms through non-violent means, negotiation, and moral persuasion. He believed in appealing to the British conscience, hoping for a fair response.
                            - "Received a stone instead"
                                - Instead of responding positively, the British government resorted to repression—passing draconian laws (like the Rowlatt Act, 1919), violent crackdowns (Jallianwala Bagh massacre), and continued economic exploitation.
                            - "The English nation responds only to force"
                                - This highlights Gandhi's realization that moral appeals alone were ineffective against colonial rulers. While he remained committed to non-violence, he recognized that mass resistance and direct action (e.g., Non-Cooperation, Civil Disobedience, Quit India Movements) were necessary to force the British to concede ground.
                - Additional Context:
                    - The Royal Commission on Indian Currency and Finance (1926), or Hilton Young Commission, recommended establishing the Reserve Bank of India (founded 1935), reflecting broader economic demands in Gandhi's ultimatum.
        - Launch of Civil Disobedience Movement (March–April 1930)
            - Preparations
                - February 1930: The CWC empowered Gandhi to choose the CDM's timing and focus. He selected the salt tax—levied since the 1882 Salt Act, affecting all classes—as a unifying symbol of British exploitation.
            - Dandi Salt March (March 12–April 6, 1930)
                - On March 2, Gandhi informed Irwin of his plan. Starting with 78 followers from Sabarmati Ashram, he marched 240 miles to Dandi, Gujarat, addressing swelling crowds en route.
                - April 6: Gandhi broke the salt law by picking up a lump of natural salt, triggering nationwide defiance as millions followed suit.
                    - Salt Act (1882)
                        - Overview
                            - The Salt Act of 1882 was a British colonial law in India that gave the government a monopoly over the production, sale, and taxation of salt. 
                            - It prohibited Indians from producing or selling salt independently, forcing them to buy heavily taxed salt from British authorities. Given salt's essential role in daily life, this law was deeply resented and symbolized British economic exploitation.
                        - Key Provisions
                            - The act restricted salt manufacture and sale to licensed producers approved by the British government.
                            - It imposed heavy taxes on salt, making it unaffordable for poor Indians.
                            - Indians were banned from freely collecting natural salt from coastal regions or salt lakes.
                            - Violation of the act led to fines and imprisonment.
                        - Impact on Indians
                            - The law disproportionately affected the poor, for whom salt was a basic necessity.
                            - It became a symbol of British economic oppression, as it forced Indians to pay excessive amounts for a natural resource.
                            - The act was one of the key triggers for nationalist resistance against British rule.
                - Symbolism: A nonviolent act of resistance, it showcased India's collective will; Subhas Chandra Bose likened it to Napoleon's march to Paris from Elba (1815), highlighting its strategic audacity.
                    - Napoleon's March to Paris from Elba (1815)
                        - Background
