                            - After his defeat in the War of the Sixth Coalition, Napoleon Bonaparte was forced to abdicate (formally renounce or give up a position of power, authority, or responsibility, especially a throne or leadership role) in April 1814 and was exiled to the small Mediterranean island of Elba. 
                            - The victorious European powers, led by Britain, Austria, Prussia, and Russia, restored the Bourbon monarchy in France under King Louis XVIII. 
                            - However, discontent grew under the new regime due to economic problems, unpopular policies, and the return of the aristocracy.
                        - The Escape from Elba
                            - On February 26, 1815, Napoleon escaped from Elba with a small force of about 1,000 men, including loyal soldiers and officers. 
                            - He landed on the French coast at Golfe-Juan (near Cannes) on March 1, 1815, beginning what is known as the Hundred Days—his dramatic return to power.
                        - The March to Paris
                            - Napoleon avoided royalist strongholds and took a route through the Alps, knowing he could win support among soldiers and peasants.
                            - At Grenoble (March 7, 1815), the first significant confrontation occurred. Instead of attacking, Napoleon walked unarmed towards the royalist troops, declaring, "If any among you will shoot his Emperor, here I am!" The soldiers joined him instead of resisting.
                            - By the time he reached Lyon (March 10, 1815), he had gathered thousands of troops, and the city welcomed him enthusiastically.
                            - King Louis XVIII sent Marshal Ney, a former Napoleonic general, to stop him. Ney initially declared loyalty to the king but, upon meeting Napoleon on March 14, 1815, famously pledged, "I would not fight against the bravest of the brave" and joined him.
                            - As Napoleon advanced, the French army and people overwhelmingly supported him, and the Bourbon monarchy collapsed without significant resistance.
                            - On March 20, 1815, Napoleon entered Paris unopposed, forcing King Louis XVIII to flee to Belgium.
                        - Impact and Consequences
                            - Napoleon's dramatic return reignited war in Europe, as the Seventh Coalition (Britain, Prussia, Austria, Russia, and others) quickly mobilized against him.
                            - He ruled France for 100 days, attempting to consolidate power and reform the army.
                            - His comeback ended at the Battle of Waterloo (June 18, 1815), where he was decisively defeated by the Duke of Wellington and Prussian forces under Blücher.
                            - After his final defeat, Napoleon was exiled to the remote island of Saint Helena, where he died in 1821.
        - Civil Disobedience Movement: Phase 1 (April–December 1930)
            - Regional Spread
                - Tamil Nadu: C. Rajagopalachari led a salt march to Vedaranniyam, bolstered by anti-liquor campaigns targeting excise revenue.
                - Malabar: K. Kelappan organized salt marches; P. Krishna Pillai faced police brutality at Calicut, amplifying defiance.
                - Andhra Pradesh: Salt protests erupted in East and West Godavari, Krishna, and Guntur districts, disrupting British salt production.
                - Bengal: Midnapur and rural areas saw salt protests and tax resistance, challenging land revenue systems.
                - Assam: Students defied the Cunningham Circular (banning political activity), sparking widespread unrest.
                - Peshawar: Khan Abdul Ghaffar Khan's Khudai Khidmatgars led protests; Garhwal Rifles soldiers refused to fire on unarmed crowds, signaling military dissent.
                - Sholapur: Workers struck, destroyed British symbols, and briefly ran a parallel government, showcasing industrial militancy.
                - Manipur and Nagaland: Rani Gaidinliu, a 13-year-old Naga leader, inspired by her cousin Haipou Jadonang, raised a revolt, blending tribal resistance with nationalism.
                - Dharasana Raid (May 21, 1930): Post-Gandhi's arrest on May 4, Sarojini Naidu, Imam Sahib, and Manilal Gandhi led a nonviolent raid on Dharasana Salt Works. Police brutality—captured by journalist Webb Miller—shocked the world, injuring over 300.
            - Mobilization Methods
                - Prabhat pheris (dawn processions), vanar senas (youth brigades), manjari senas (women's brigades), secret patrikas (pamphlets), and magic lantern shows spread awareness and morale.
                - Forest Law Violations: Central Provinces and Karnataka saw defiance of British forest monopolies, reclaiming resources.
                - No-Tax Campaigns: Gujarat villagers migrated to princely states like Baroda to evade taxes, disrupting revenue collection.
            - Government Response
                - Repression: June 1930 ordinances—Prevention of Intimidation and Unlawful Instigation—banned Congress affiliates, imposed press censorship, and arrested over 60,000 by year-end.
                    - During the Civil Disobedience Movement (CDM) in 1930, the British government, under Viceroy Lord Irwin, responded with severe repression. A series of ordinances were issued in June 1930 to curb the movement's momentum, including the Prevention of Intimidation and Unlawful Instigation Ordinance.
                    - These ordinances aimed to suppress nationalist activities, particularly those involving mass mobilization, boycotts, and civil disobedience.
                    - Key Features of the Ordinance:
                        - Restrictions on Public Gatherings 
                            - Prohibited large meetings, particularly those instigating defiance against the British authorities.
                        - Criminalization of Intimidation 
                            - Any form of coercion, whether verbal threats or physical intimidation, against government officials or those refusing to participate in the movement was penalized.
                        - Censorship and Press Suppression 
                            - Nationalist newspapers and pamphlets that encouraged resistance were banned or heavily censored.
                        - Arrests Without Trial 
                            - Authorities were given the power to detain individuals indefinitely without formal charges.
                        - Confiscation of Property 
                            - Properties of movement leaders and active participants could be seized.
                    - Impact of the Ordinance:
                        - Widespread Arrests: 
                            - Prominent leaders, including Mahatma Gandhi, were arrested, leading to an escalation of tensions.
                        - Increased Repression: 
                            - British officials used force to disperse protests, often leading to violence and further radicalization of the movement.
                        - Public Defiance: 
                            - Despite the harsh measures, civil disobedience persisted, with volunteers continuing salt satyagrahas, picketing liquor shops, and boycotting foreign goods.
                        - International Condemnation: 
                            - The crackdown attracted global criticism, increasing sympathy for the Indian cause, particularly in Britain and the United States.
                - Negotiation Attempt: July 1930, Irwin proposed a Round Table Conference emphasizing Dominion Status. Liberals Tej Bahadur Sapru and M.R. Jayakar mediated, meeting Gandhi, Motilal, and Jawaharlal Nehru in Yerwada Jail in August, but Congress demands—independence, defense control, and an independent tribunal—were unmet.
        - First Round Table Conference (November 12, 1930–January 19, 1931)
            - Participants: British officials, princely states, and minorities (Muslims, Sikhs, depressed classes); Congress and major business leaders boycotted it.
            - Outcomes: Agreed on an All-India Federation, provincial autonomy, and Dr. B.R. Ambedkar's push for separate electorates for depressed classes, but lacked Congress input, limiting legitimacy.
        - Gandhi-Irwin Pact (March 5, 1931)
            - Negotiation Process
                - Liberal Appeal: Indian Liberal Party members—Tej Bahadur Sapru, C.Y. Chintamani, Srinivasa Sastri—urged Gandhi to negotiate post-First RTC failure.
                - Releases: January 25, 1931, Gandhi and CWC members were freed unconditionally; the CWC authorized Gandhi to meet Irwin.
                - Talks: February 17–March 5, Gandhi and Irwin held eight meetings, culminating in the pact.
            - British Concessions (a thing that is granted, especially in response to demands)
                - Immediate release of non-violent political prisoners.
                - Remission of uncollected fines.
                - Return of confiscated lands not sold to third parties.
                - Lenient reinstatement for resigned government servants.
                - Permission for coastal villages to make salt for personal use (not sale).
                - Right to peaceful picketing of liquor and foreign goods shops.
                - Withdrawal of emergency ordinances.
            - Rejected Demands
                - No public inquiry into police excesses, despite widespread brutality reports.
                - Refusal to commute Bhagat Singh, Rajguru, and Sukhdev's death sentences (executed March 23, 1931), despite Gandhi's plea, sparking public outrage.
            - Congress Commitments
                - Suspend CDM, attend the Second Round Table Conference, and halt boycotts temporarily.
        - Karachi Special Congress Session (March 26–31, 1931)
            - Presided by Sardar Vallabhbhai Patel, reflecting his rising stature.
            - Key Developments:
                - Ratified (to officially approve, confirm, or validate a decision, treaty or agreement) the Gandhi-Irwin Pact, despite dissent over Bhagat Singh's execution.
                - Adopted Fundamental Rights and National Economic Programme (drafted by Nehru with M.N. Roy), outlining civil liberties and economic justice.
                - Condemned political violence but admired martyrs' sacrifices, balancing Gandhi's non-violence with revolutionary sentiment.
                - Reaffirmed Poorna Swaraj as the goal.
            - Significance: First clear articulation of Swaraj's socio-economic dimensions for the masses.
        - Second Round Table Conference (September 7–December 1, 1931)
            - Participants: Congress joined, with Gandhi, Madan Mohan Malaviya, Sarojini Naidu, G.D. Birla, and others, alongside Muslim League, Hindu Mahasabha, and Ambedkar for depressed classes.
            - Challenges:
                - Shift from Labour to National Government in Britain diluted focus; Irwin's replacement, Lord Willingdon, adopted a hardline stance.
                - Communal issues dominated, culminating in a Minorities' Pact for separate electorates, deepening divisions.
            - Outcome: No agreement; Prime Minister Ramsay MacDonald announced a unilateral Communal Award (August 1932), establishing NWFP and Sindh as Muslim-majority provinces and forming advisory committees on finance, franchise, and federation.
        - Civil Disobedience Movement: Phase 2 (January 1932–April 1934)
            - Relaunch
                - December 29, 1931: Post-Second RTC failure, the CWC relaunched CDM amid (surrounded by; in the middle of) Willingdon's refusal to negotiate further.
                - January 4, 1932: Gandhi was arrested; Congress organizations were banned, properties like Sabarmati Ashram seized, and media curbs tightened.
            - Public Reaction
                - [Your opinion placeholder: As per your instruction, list your view here first.]
                - Nationwide protests erupted, with over 120,000 arrests by 1933; rural defiance persisted via no-tax campaigns and urban picketing, though momentum waned under repression.
            - Government Crackdown
                - Willingdon's regime intensified arrests, banned Congress nationwide, and suppressed dissent, contrasting Irwin's conciliatory (intended or likely to placate or pacify) approach.
        - Third Round Table Conference (November 17–December 24, 1932)
            - Limited Participation: Minimal Indian representation; Congress boycotted, rendering it ineffective.
            - Outcome: Produced a White Paper (March 1933) outlining constitutional reforms, criticized for lacking Indian input.
        - Government of India Act, 1935
            - Background
                - Based on the Third RTC's White Paper, it aimed to appease Indian demands while retaining British control, facing universal criticism from Congress and others for falling short of independence.
            - Key Provisions
                - All-India Federation: 
                    - Proposed uniting British provinces and princely states (never implemented, as states' accession failed).
                - Provincial Autonomy: 
                    - Granted provinces control over all departments via elected governments, free from GOI oversight except in emergencies, with governors retaining ordinance (an authoritative order) powers.
                - Dyarchy at Center: 
                    - Reserved subjects (defense, foreign affairs) under Governor General's counselors; transferred subjects (e.g., education) with ministers answerable to legislature.
                - Safeguards: 
                    - Vested special powers in executives to override legislatures, ensuring British dominance.
            - Structural Changes
                - National Level:
                    - Federation required states to fill half of 52 Council of States seats and represent 50% of princely populations—conditions unmet by 1939.
                    - Abolished the Indian Council (1858), long a target of nationalist ire (ire).
                        - The Indian Councils Acts (1861, 1892, 1909):
                            - These were Acts passed by the British Parliament to change the structure and functions of the councils in India. They are often collectively referred to when discussing constitutional reforms.
                            - Act of 1861: 
                                - This act marked a beginning for representative institutions by allowing the Viceroy to nominate Indians as non-official members to his expanded Legislative Council. 
                                - It also restored legislative powers to the Bombay and Madras presidencies (decentralization) and introduced the portfolio system for the Viceroy's Executive Council.
                            - Act of 1892: 
                                - This act increased the number of non-official members in the Central and Provincial Legislative Councils. 
                                - It introduced a limited system of indirect election (members recommended by local bodies like municipalities, district boards, universities, etc.) and gave the councils the power to discuss the budget and ask questions to the executive.
                            - Act of 1909 (Morley-Minto Reforms): 
                                - Further expanded the legislative councils and introduced separate electorates for Muslims, significantly impacting Indian politics. 
                                - It also allowed members to ask supplementary questions and move resolutions.
                - Central Government:
                    - Bicameral (divided into two separate assemblies, chambers, or houses) legislature—Council of States (upper house) and Federal Assembly (lower house)—with princely states overrepresented to counter nationalists.
                - Provincial Government:
                    - Ended provincial dyarchy (government by two independent authorities), establishing fully responsible governments subject to governors' safeguards.
                    - Introduced bicameral legislatures in Bengal, Madras, Bombay, United Provinces, Bihar, and Assam.
                - Other Features:
                    - Created Burma as a separate entity (1937).
                    - Lowered franchise but tied it to property, limiting voters to 14% of the population.
                    - Extended separate electorates to women, following Ambedkar's advocacy.
                    - Established Federal Bank (Reserve Bank of India, 1935), Federal Court (1937), and Federal Railway Authority, insulated from ministerial control.
            - Critique: Lacked a preamble defining self-governance goals, reinforcing British intent to delay full independence.    

            
- British Acts and Regulations (1858-1935)
    - GOI Act, 1858
        - Background
            - Key Context
                - The Revolt of 1857 dealt a fatal blow to the existing governance system, accelerating demands for transferring power from the East India Company to the Crown.
                - Lord Palmerston (British PM) first introduced a bill for this transfer.
                - Later, Edward Henry Stanley (the first Secretary of State for India) introduced “An Act for the Better Government of India,” passed on August 2, 1858.
                - 
        - Main Provisions
            - Power Transfer
                - Ended the Company's rule in India, transferring power to the Crown.
                - Abolished the Court of Directors and Board of Control, shifting their powers to the Secretary of State for India and a 15-member Indian Council.
                    - The Indian Council assisted and advised the Secretary of State, governing India in the Crown's name. All 15 members were British (not Indians).
                    - The council operated from London, UK. It was part of the India Office (located at London), which handled British governance over India.
                    - The Secretary of State, a British Cabinet member, was accountable to Parliament and required to submit periodic reports on India's moral and material progress.
                    - The Secretary of State was declared a corporate body, capable of suing and being sued in England and India.
                - The Governor General was titled Viceroy, becoming the Crown's direct representative.
        - Significance
            - Impact of the Act
                - Marked the end of Company rule, though the power transfer was more formal than substantial.
                    - The name change aimed to erase past grievances and offer a fresh start for the empire.
                - Followed by Queen Victoria's Proclamation on November 1, 1858, at a grand Durbar in Allahabad, known as the “Magna Carta of the People of India,” outlining future British policy in India.

    - Indian Councils Act, 1861
        - Background
            - Historical Context
                - The Regulating Act initiated centralization, culminating in the 1833 Act, which stripped provinces of legislative powers. The 1861 Act reversed this trend, starting decentralization.
                - Lord Canning (Governor General) and Sir Charles Wood (Secretary of State) facilitated its passage.
        - Main Provisions
            - Changes in Governance
                - Changes in the Viceroy's Executive Council
                    - Expanded by adding a fifth member, a jurist.
                - Changes in the Central Legislature
                    - Size and Composition
                        - Enlarged by adding 6-12 additional members, nominated by the Governor General for two years, with at least half being non-officials. Legislative functions were strictly limited to law-making.
                    - Functions
                        - Restored legislative powers to Bombay and Madras Councils, though laws required the Governor General's assent, imposing rigid restrictions.
                - Other Changes Introduced
                    - Allowed establishment of new councils in provinces like North Western Provinces (1886), Burma, and Punjab (1897).
                    - Empowered the Governor General to issue ordinances in emergencies (valid for six months) without Legislative Council concurrence.
                    - Authorized the Governor General to create new provinces, appoint Lieutenant Governors, and adjust territorial boundaries.
                    - Decentralization of Business
                        - The Governor General could make rules for efficient council transactions.
                        - Lord Canning introduced the portfolio system, dividing departments among council members, laying the foundation for India's cabinet system.
        - Limitations
            - Legislative Constraints
                - No clear demarcation of legislative powers between Central and Local Legislatures; the Governor General retained authority to legislate for all of India.

    - Indian Councils Act, 1892
        - Main Provisions
            - Legislative Adjustments
                - Changes in the Central Legislature
                    - Increased additional members to 10-16, with at least half non-officials.
                    - Provided for 8-12 non-official members in Provincial Legislatures, though official majorities were maintained centrally and provincially.
                - Other Changes
                    - Empowered the Governor General to introduce limited elections.
                        - Regulations for nominating additional members were set by the Governor General-in-Council, though nominees were not representatives but appointees.
                        - Introduced a cautious, indirect election system, avoiding the term “election.”
                    - Expanded Functions
                        - Members could discuss the annual financial statement (unalterable) and ask questions on public interest matters with six days' notice, though supplementary questions were barred.
            - Criticism
                - The Indian National Congress criticized the Act in its 1892 and 1893 sessions for failing to introduce direct elections.

    - Indian Councils Act of 1909 (Morley-Minto Reforms)
        - Background
            - Political Climate
                - Introduced amid rising political unrest and dissatisfaction with British rule.
                - The 1906 British Liberal Party victory raised reform prospects, believing Indian legislative participation would strengthen British control.
                - The 1906 Shimla Deputation, led by Aga Khan, proposed separate Muslim electorates to Lord Minto.
        - Main Provisions
            - Structural Reforms
                - Indian Entry in Viceroy's Executive Council
                    - Satyendra Sinha became the first Indian appointed in 1909.
                - Expansion of Members
                    - Increased elected members in Imperial and Provincial Legislative Councils, with non-official majorities in provinces.
                    - Central Legislative Council: 69 members (37 officials, 5 nominated non-officials, 27 elected non-officials), with seats for Muslims, British capitalists, landlords, and a general electorate.
                    - Emphasized representation by classes and interests over territorial representation.
                - Legislative Powers
                    - Members could discuss financial statements, move resolutions (as recommendations only), and ask supplementary questions, though some topics (e.g., foreign relations) were restricted.
                - Introduction of Elections
                    - Indirect elections for non-officials, based on class and community, with recognized bodies recommending candidates.
                - Provision of Separate Electorates
                    - Introduced for Muslims in the Central Legislative Council, with overrepresentation and low-income voting qualifications serving British strategic interests.

    - Government of India Act of 1919 (Montagu-Chelmsford Reforms)
        - Background
            - Foundational Declaration
                - Based on the Montagu Declaration of August 20, 1917, promising increased Indian administrative participation and gradual self-governance within the British Empire.
        - Provisions
            - Governance Changes
                - Changes in Home Government
                    - Appointed a High Commissioner for India in London for trade, funded by Indian revenue, not the British Exchequer.
                - Changes in Government of India
                    - Viceroy's Executive Council
                        - Three of eight members were to be Indian, aligning with the policy of Indian association.
                    - Bicameral System
                        - Replaced the single Legislative Council with the Council of States (Upper House) and Legislative Assembly (Lower House), with elected members under limited franchise.
                    - Expansion of Functions
                        - All members could ask supplementary and short-notice questions, enjoying freedom of speech.
                - Changes in Provincial Governments
                    - Provincial Autonomy
                        - Introduced via “Devolution Rules,” defining independent provincial powers in finance, legislation, and administration.
                    - Legislative Devolution
                        - Divided legislative items into Central and Provincial Lists, with residual powers vested in the Governor General-in-Council.
                        - Provincial List split into “Transferred” (managed by Indian ministers accountable to the Legislative Council) and “Reserved” subjects (managed by Governors and their Executive Councils), creating dyarchy.
                    - Financial Devolution
                        - Separated revenue sources: land revenue, excise, irrigation, and stamps became provincial; budgets were split from the center, with provinces authorized to frame their own.
                - Other Provisions
                    - Introduced direct elections with limited franchise.
                    - Extended communal representation to Sikhs, Indian Christians, Anglo-Indians, and Europeans, alongside Muslims.
                    - Established the Central Public Service Commission (1926) for civil servant recruitment.
                    - Provided for a Statutory (required, permitted, or enacted by statute (legislative body)) Commission to review the Act after 10 years.
        - Points to Know
            - Additional Insights
                - Initially applied to eight provinces (Bengal, Madras, Bombay, UP, Punjab, Bihar, Orissa, Central Provinces), extended to Burma (1923) and NWFP (North-West Frontier Provinc) later.
                - Women could neither vote nor contest elections, reflecting Britain's own post-1918 suffrage timeline.
                - The Muddiman Report analyzed dyarchy's implementation.

    - Simon Commission and Its Aftermath (1927–1929)
        - Overview
            - The Simon Commission, formally the Indian Statutory Commission, was appointed in November 1927 to review the Government of India (GOI) Act 1919, sparking widespread Indian resistance due to its all-British composition. 
            - Its arrival in 1928 catalyzed political unity efforts, notably the Nehru Report, and intensified demands for independence, setting the stage for the Civil Disobedience Movement.
        - Formation and Context of the Simon Commission
            - Background
                - The GOI Act 1919 mandated a decennial review of constitutional progress. In 1927, Britain's Conservative government under Stanley Baldwin preempted a potential Labour-led review by advancing it to November 8, fearing more progressive reforms.
                - Named after its chairman, Sir John Simon, the seven-member, all-white commission excluded Indians, justified by its accountability to the British Parliament rather than Indian stakeholders.
                - Appointed amid rising nationalist activity, including the Swaraj Party's legislative disruptions and youth movements led by Jawaharlal Nehru and Subhas Chandra Bose.
            - Purpose
                - Assessed India's readiness for further self-governance under the 1919 Act, focusing on dyarchy's effectiveness and administrative structures, amid growing demands for Dominion Status or independence.
            - Preceding Reports
                - Lee Commission (1924): Investigated racial disparities in the Imperial Civil Services, recommending increased Indian recruitment.
                - Muddiman Commission (1924): Examined dyarchy's operational flaws, suggesting minor adjustments rather than overhaul.
                - Linlithgow Commission (1926): Addressed agricultural crises and rural economy, highlighting peasant distress as a nationalist rallying point.
        - Indian Reactions to the Simon Commission
            - Political Opposition and Boycotts
                - Congress Response
                    - At the Madras Session (December 1927), presided by M.A. Ansari, Congress resolved to boycott the commission “at every stage and in every form,” reflecting outrage at Indian exclusion.
                    - Jawaharlal Nehru advocated complete independence over Dominion Status, galvanizing radical youth.
                    - Congress launched the All Parties Conference to draft an alternative constitution, seeking cross-party unity.
                - Broader Opposition
                    - Hindu Mahasabha, Liberal Federation (led by Tej Bahadur Sapru), Jinnah's Muslim League faction, and the Indian Industrial and Commercial Congress joined the boycott, decrying the commission as unrepresentative.
                - Supporters
                    - Unionists in Punjab (landlord-dominated), Justice Party in South India (non-Brahmin elites), and All India Achyut Federation (pro-British untouchable groups) endorsed it, aligning with colonial interests.
            - Delhi Proposals (December 1927)
                - Unity Initiative
                    - Presided by Jinnah, this Muslim League-led effort aimed to align Congress and Muslim demands:
                        - Joint electorates with reserved Muslim seats.
                        - One-third Muslim representation in the Central Legislative Assembly.
                        - Proportional representation in Punjab and Bengal.
                        - Creation of Muslim-majority provinces: Sindh, Balochistan, North-West Frontier Province (NWFP).
                    - Congress accepted most terms, but internal Muslim League splits (e.g., Shafi faction's dissent) and Hindu Mahasabha's rejection of concessions derailed unity.
        - All Parties Conference and the Nehru Report (1928)
            - Establishment
                - Convened in February 1928 by M.A. Ansari, it included Congress, Muslim League, Hindu Mahasabha, Sikh Central League, and others, agreeing on Dominion Status as a compromise goal.
                - Resulted in the Nehru Report (released August 1928, finalized May), India's first indigenous constitutional draft, chaired by Motilal Nehru.
            - Key Recommendations of Nehru Report
                - Constitutional Framework
                    - Dominion Status: India as a self-governing Commonwealth member.
                    - Fundamental Rights: Universal adult suffrage, equal rights (religion, speech, gender), and legal equality.
                    - Minority Safeguards: Joint electorates with reserved seats for minorities; special status for Muslim-majority provinces (e.g., Sindh separated from Bombay, NWFP granted provincial status).
                    - Provincial Reorganization: Linguistic basis for provinces, enhancing regional identity.
                    - Federal Structure: Centralized federal government with provincial autonomy, residual powers retained centrally (more unitary than federal in practice).
                    - Responsible Government: Bicameral legislature (upper and lower houses) and a judicial hierarchy culminating in a supreme court.
                - Additional Provisions
                    - Balochistan elevated to full province status; communal representation to be reviewed after 20 years.
                - Princely States Dilemma
                    - The Nehru Committee grappled with integrating Princely States. 
                    - The 1927 States People Conference demanded self-governance, but the Butler Committee (1927–1928, under Sir Harcourt Butler) reinforced British paramountcy, preserving princely autonomy under Crown suzerainty.
            - British Perspective
                - Lord Birkenhead, Secretary of State, defended Indian exclusion, citing irreconcilable political divisions and challenging Indians to produce a unified constitution—a dare that spurred the Nehru Report.
        - Opposition and Divisions
            - Political Reactions
                - Muslim League (Jinnah faction), Hindu Mahasabha, and Sikh League opposed the Nehru Report, rejecting its unitary tilt and inadequate communal safeguards.
                - Jinnah's Amendments (Rejected):
                    - One-third Muslim representation in the central legislature.
                    - Proportional representation in Punjab and Bengal.
                    - Residual powers to provinces, favoring federalism over centralization.
                - Outcome: Jinnah's 14 Points (March 1929) emerged as a counterproposal, shaping Muslim League's future separatism.
            - Compromises in Nehru Report
                - Final Adjustments: Adopted joint electorates except in Muslim-majority areas; Sindh separation contingent on Dominion Status; retained centralized residual powers.
                - Submission: Approved by the All Parties Conference without Jinnah's amendments, signaling partial unity but exposing communal fissures.
        - Calcutta Congress Session (December 1928)
            - Internal Dynamics
                - Presided by Motilal Nehru, the session endorsed the Nehru Report but faced dissent from younger leaders—Jawaharlal Nehru, Subhas Chandra Bose, and S. Satyamurthy—who rejected Dominion Status for complete independence.
                - Independence of India League: Formed April 1928 by Nehru, Bose (Secretary), and S. Srinivasa Iyengar (President), it pushed for radical action over constitutional compromise.
            - Ultimatum
                - Congress set a one-year deadline (until December 31, 1929) for Britain to grant Dominion Status, threatening civil disobedience for Poorna Swaraj if unmet—a decisive shift toward mass agitation.
        - Outcomes and Legacy
            - Simon Commission's Reception
                - Arrived in India on February 3, 1928, met with nationwide protests—“Simon Go Back” slogans, black flags, and hartals—reflecting unified Indian rejection.
                - Submitted its report in May 1930, recommending provincial autonomy, dyarchy abolition in provinces, and a federal framework, but ignored by Congress due to its exclusionary process.
            - Catalyst for Unity and Radicalization
                - Spurred the Nehru Report as a nationalist counter-narrative, though its failure to bridge communal divides foreshadowed future tensions.
                - Pushed Congress toward Poorna Swaraj at the Lahore Session (December 1929), launching the Civil Disobedience Movement in 1930.

    - Government of India Act of 1935
        - 
        - Background
            - Policy Foundation
                - Based on the White Paper on the Indian Constitution post-Third Round Table Conference, criticized by all political parties for falling short of complete independence.
        - Main Provisions
            - Structural Reforms
                - Contained four major provisions: All India Federation, Provincial Autonomy, dyarchy at the center, and safeguards vesting special powers with central and provincial executives.
                - Provided legal safeguards against misbehavior by Indian ministers and legislators; notably lacked a preamble.
                - Changes at National Level
                    - Proposal for an All India Federation (never materialized)
                        - Voluntary accession for states, requiring at least half of the 52 allotted Council of States seats and 50% of the states' population to join.
                    - Abolished the Indian Council (from the 1858 Act) due to anti-India policy agitations.
                - Changes in Government of India
                    - Dyarchy at the Centre
                        - Introduced dyarchy centrally: Reserved Departments under the Governor General with appointed counselors; Transferred subjects advised by ministers answerable to the legislature.
                    - Bicameral Central Legislature
                        - Comprised the Governor General, Council of States, and Federal Assembly, with representation from British India and Princely States (disproportionate weightage for states as nationalist checks).
                - Changes in Provincial Government
                    - Provincial Autonomy
                        - All departments controlled by elected members, provinces recognized as separate legal entities deriving power from the Crown, free from GOI (Government Of India) and SOS (Secretary of State) control except for specific purposes.
                        - Governors could issue ordinances or enact Governor's Acts.
                    - Abolition of Dyarchy in Provinces
                        - Eliminated the distinction between Reserved and Transferred subjects, establishing fully responsible government with safeguards.
                    - Bicameral Legislature
                        - Introduced in six provinces: Bengal, Madras, Bombay, United Provinces, Bihar, and Assam.
                - Other Provisions
                    - Created new provinces (e.g., Burma separated from India).
                    - Lowered franchise, though tied to property qualifications.
                    - Extended separate electorates to women.
                    - Established Federal Bank, Federal Court (1935, 1937), and Federal Railway Authority (free from ministerial control).


- Indian National Movement Post CDM (1935-1942) - Legislative Engagement & Growing Radicalism phase
    - 
    - Understanding Depressed Classes Politics in the 1900s
        - Introduction
            - The early 20th century marked a transformative period for the Depressed Classes in India. British colonialism opened new avenues for social mobility, challenging the rigid caste hierarchy.
            - Awareness of rights grew among the Depressed Classes, intensifying tensions with caste Hindus.
            - Representation and equality emerged as central issues, shaped by key leaders and policies in their fight for political and social justice.
        - Key Developments: The Minorities Pact
            - Context
                - During the Round Table Conference (RTC), Dr. B.R. Ambedkar initially supported joint electorates with reservations but shifted his stance when his demand for universal adult suffrage (right to vote) was rejected.
                - He aligned with Muslim, Anglo-Indian, and Christian representatives to form the Minorities Pact.
            - Provisions
                - Proposed separate electorates for the Depressed Classes, with a referendum planned after 20 years to reassess the arrangement.
            - Reason for Shift
                - Ambedkar's universal adult suffrage proposal was opposed by the Muslim League, princely states, and the British government, which hesitated to adopt it.
                - Without universal suffrage, Dalit representatives risked being elected mainly by caste Hindus, leaving their political influence stagnant (motionless).
        - Key Developments: The Rajah-Moonje Pact (1932)
            - 
            - Overview (No Need to Memorize)
                - In 1932, the Hindu Mahasabha, aiming to preserve Hindu unity, collaborated with M.C. Rajah.
                - Provisions included reserved seats for Dalits within joint electorates to promote unity while consolidating Hindu interests.
                - Impact: Rajah's shift from supporting separate electorates to joint electorates split Dalit opinion, reflecting the evolving political landscape and lack of consensus among Dalit leaders.
        - The Communal Award (August 16, 1932)
            - Background
                - Announced by British PM Ramsay MacDonald, based on the Lothian Committee (Indian Franchise Committee) recommendations after the Second RTC, to ensure representation for minorities and marginalized groups, including Muslims, Sikhs, Christians, Anglo-Indians, Europeans, and Depressed Classes.
            - Lothian Committee Recommendations (For Understanding)
                - Expansion of electorates for provincial and federal assemblies.
                - Reservation of 2-5% of seats for women.
                - Specific provisions treating Depressed Classes as a distinct minority.
                - Left representation decisions for princely states to the states themselves.
            - Key Provisions
                - Allocated separate electorates for Muslims, Sikhs, Christians, Anglo-Indians, Europeans, and Depressed Classes.
                - Reserved 71 seats for Depressed Classes in provincial legislatures (later raised to 148 under Gandhi) and 18% of total seats in the central legislature.
                - Provided weightage to Muslims in minority provinces.
                - Doubled the number of seats in provincial legislatures for better representation.
                - Reserved 3% of seats for women in most provinces.
                - Allotted seven seats for Marathas in Bombay.
                - Included educational grants to improve facilities for Depressed Classes.
            - Responses
                - Congress: Opposed separate electorates but avoided altering the Award without minority consent, adopting a stance of neither acceptance nor rejection.
                - Gandhi: Saw it as a threat to Indian unity, opposed segregating Depressed Classes politically, and launched an indefinite fast to demand joint electorates with reserved seats.
        - Poona Pact (September 1932)
            - Agreement signed between Mahatma Gandhi and Dr. B.R. Ambedkar at Yerwada Jail, Poona, to resolve the dispute over separate electorates for Depressed Classes.
            - Key Provisions
                - Increased reserved seats for Depressed Classes from 71 to 148 in provincial legislatures and maintained 18% in the central legislature.
                - Established joint electorates with reserved seats for Depressed Classes.
                - Introduced a primary election system, where an electoral college of Depressed Class candidates would finalize nominations.
                - Provided educational grants and ensured no disabilities for Depressed Classes.
                - Made the reservation system subject to future mutual agreement.
            - Outcome
                - Amended the Communal Award and was approved by the British government.

    - Post-CDM and the Transition to Constitutional Politics
        - End of Civil Disobedience Movement (CDM)
            - In April 1934, Gandhi withdrew the CDM, shifting from mass struggles to a phase of non-mass political activity.
            - Three perspectives emerged on the next steps:
                - Gandhian Constructive Work: Focused on Harijan upliftment and rural reconstruction.
                - Constitutional Struggle: Advocated by M.A. Ansari and Asaf Ali for participating in elections and working within legislative frameworks.
                - Leftist Perspective: Nehru and others pushed for continued non-constitutional mass movements, criticizing Gandhi's “Struggle-Truce-Struggle” approach and favoring “Struggle-Victory.”
                    - Gandhi's "Struggle-Truce-Struggle" Approach
                        - Gandhi believed in alternating between mass movements and negotiations to achieve constitutional concessions.
                        - Examples:
                            - Non-Cooperation Movement (1920-22): Ended abruptly after the Chauri Chaura incident.
                            - Civil Disobedience Movement (1930-34): Suspended after the Gandhi-Irwin Pact.
                            - Quit India Movement (1942): A final push after failed negotiations with the British.
                            - Gandhi's strategy aimed at gradual progress through moral and political pressure rather than an all-out confrontation.
                    - Leftist "Struggle-Victory" Approach
                        - Nehru, Bose, and other left-leaning leaders argued for unrelenting struggle until complete victory, rejecting temporary compromises.
                        - Their key beliefs:
                            - The British would never concede independence voluntarily through negotiations.
                            - Movements should not pause for political deals but should escalate until colonial rule was completely dismantled.
                            - Greater emphasis on mass mobilization, socialism, and direct action.
                            - This led to tensions within the Congress, as younger and more radical leaders like Subhas Chandra Bose and Nehru often found Gandhi's methods too conservative or cautious.
                    - Impact of This Divide
                        - The leftist faction influenced Congress towards a more assertive stance, culminating in the 1930 Purna Swaraj resolution and the Quit India Movement (1942).  
                        - Subhas Chandra Bose, who strongly opposed any truce with the British, eventually left Congress to lead the Indian National Army (INA) for armed struggle.
                        - Nehru, though critical of truce-based strategies, remained within the Congress framework and later shaped India’s socialist-leaning post-independence policies.
        - Understanding These Factions: Neo-Swarajists
            - Council Entry
                - Congress leaders, dubbed Neo-Swarajists, advocated participating in the 1934 Central Legislative Assembly (CLA) elections.
                - In 1933, S. Satyamurthy founded the Madras Swaraj Party to support council entry.
                - C. Rajagopalachari pushed for council entry to broaden Congress's political base and prepare for future mass struggles.
                - Unlike earlier Swarajists, they aimed for cooperation in legislative councils, including assuming office and forming ministries.
        - Understanding These Factions: Socialists
            - Class Struggle
                - Led by Jawaharlal Nehru, Subhas Chandra Bose, and Jayaprakash Narayan, socialists within Congress focused on:
                    - Mobilizing workers and peasants for a more equitable society.
                    - Nationalizing key industries and resources and undertaking land reforms.
                    - Establishing a planned economy.
                - They opposed council entry, believing it diverted focus from the anti-imperialist struggle, with Nehru strongly against participating in elections.
        - Gandhi Resigns from Congress (1934)
            - By 1934, Gandhi felt out of sync with Congress currents:
                - Many favored parliamentary politics, which he opposed.
                - His emphasis on spinning and Harijan work alienated some workers.
                - Nehru's socialist group was gaining influence.
            - In October 1934, Gandhi resigned from Congress to serve better through thought, word, and deed.
        - Shift to Constitutional Politics
            - May 1934 (AICC at Patna): Congress established a Parliamentary Board to contest upcoming elections.
            - November 1934 (CLA Elections): Congress won 45 of 75 seats reserved for Indians, becoming the largest party and marking a shift from confrontation to constitutional politics.
        - Congress Nationalist Party
            - Formed by M.M. Malaviya and Madhav Shrihari Aney, who split from Congress to protest the 1932 Communal Award. After the 1934 elections, Congress and the Nationalist Party together held a majority in the CLA.
            - 
        - Lucknow Session (April 1936)
            - President: Jawaharlal Nehru.
            - Key Outcomes:
                - Highlighted socialism as vital for global and Indian issues, with three socialists—Jayaprakash Narayan, Acharya Narendra Dev, and Achyut Patwardhan—in the CWC.
                - Resolved to contest elections based on an election manifesto.
                - Advocated equal rights for princely state residents, matching the rest of India.
            - Left the question of office acceptance pending.
        - Faizpur Congress Session (December 1936) - First Rural Session
            - President: Jawaharlal Nehru.
            - Key Outcomes:
                - Nehru condemned fascism (far-right, authoritarian, and ultranationalist political ideology).
                - Called for a Constituent Assembly to draft India's constitution.
                - Adopted an Agrarian Programme:
                    - Reduced land tax and rent by 50%.
                    - Recognized peasant unions (Kisan Sabhas).
                    - Aimed to abolish taxes paid to landlords completely.
        - 1937 Provincial Elections
            - Held in eleven provinces under the GOI Act 1935; Congress won majorities in seven, forming ministries except in Bengal, Punjab, and Sindh.
            - Performed poorly in Upper House elections due to a limited franchise favoring landlords and moneylenders.
            - Note: CLA (Central Legislative Assembly) elections of 1934 were under the GOI Act 1919, while 1937 provincial elections followed the GOI Act 1935.
            - 
        - Question of Office Acceptance (1937)
            - March 1937 (AICC Meeting, Presided by Rajendra Prasad): Conditionally accepted office if governors refrained from using special powers under the GOI Act 1935 to interfere with ministries.
            - June 1937: Viceroy clarified governors would not intervene in government affairs regarding special powers.
            - July 1937 (CWC at Wardha): Authorized office acceptance.
            - Congress formed a Parliamentary Sub-Committee (Sardar Patel, Maulana Abul Kalam Azad, Rajendra Prasad) to prevent British divide-and-rule tactics.
            - Resulted in 28 months of Congress rule focused on public welfare and reforms.
            - Gandhi in Harijan: “These offices have to be held lightly and not tightly. They are crowns of thorns.”
                - Analysis of this quote
                    - This reflects his philosophy on leadership, power, and public service.
                        - Leadership as a Responsibility, Not a Privilege
                        - Detachment from Authority
                        - Critique of Power-Hungry Politics
            - Note: Leftists opposed office acceptance as collaboration with imperialism, while right-wing leaders like C. Rajagopalachari supported it for enabling peasant and worker reforms.
        - Key Developments During Congress Rule (1937-1939)
            - Administrative Reforms
                - Repealed emergency laws (e.g., Public Safety Act) and lifted press restrictions.
                - Released political prisoners and removed bans on groups like Hindustan Seva Dal (Communist Party ban remained).
            - Social Reforms
                - Promoted Harijan welfare through temple entry and scholarships.
                - Expanded educational facilities and public health initiatives.
            - Economic Reforms
                - Encouraged khadi production with subsidies.
                - Imposed prohibition in certain areas.
            - Labour Reforms
                - Mediated trade union protests (e.g., Industrial Disputes Bill 1938).
            - Agrarian Focus
                - Adopted a radical agrarian program at the 1936 Faizpur Session.
        - Resignation of Congress Ministries (October 1939)
            - Congress ministries resigned due to India's involvement in World War II without consultation.
            
    - Subhas Chandra Bose and Ideological Divides
        - Early Political Journey
            - 1921: Joined the Non-Cooperation Movement (NCM), mentored by C.R. Das; edited *Forward*, the Swaraj Party’s newspaper, amplifying its radical voice.
            - 1930: Founded the Congress Democratic Party within Congress; elected president of the All India Trade Union Congress (AITUC), championing labor rights during the Civil Disobedience Movement (CDM).
            - 1933–1936: Exiled to Europe after CDM imprisonment; returned in 1936, invigorated by socialist ideas from abroad.
        - Haripura Congress Session (February 1938)
            - Elected Congress president, Bose saw Congress ministries (formed post-1937 elections) as revolutionary platforms.
            - Advocated economic planning, establishing the National Planning Committee under Jawaharlal Nehru to draft industrial policies.
            - Resolutions included:
                - Vision of a Socialist State in India.
                - Abolition of landlordism (zamindari system).
                - Protection of workers’ rights and welfare for peasants and farmers.
            - Sent a medical mission to aid Chinese forces against Japanese aggression, reflecting anti-imperialist solidarity.
        - Ideological Clash with Congress Leadership
            - Bose criticized senior leaders in *The Indian Struggle* (1935), labeling them “Rightists” for compromising with Britain via the Government of India Act 1935’s federal scheme, questioning their nationalist commitment.
            - Congress leaders struggled to collaborate with Bose’s confrontational stance, deepening internal divides.
        - Tripuri Congress Session (March 1939)
            - Re-elected president, defeating Gandhi’s nominee, Pattabhi Sitaramayya (1,581 vs. 1,375 votes), despite Gandhi’s opposition.
            - Gandhi called Sitaramayya’s loss “my own defeat,” signaling a personal rift.
            - Bose proposed a six-month ultimatum to Britain for independence, favoring immediate struggle; Gandhi opposed, citing unpreparedness of Congress and the masses.
        - Resignation and Forward Bloc
            - April 29, 1939: Resigned as president due to lack of CWC support; Rajendra Prasad succeeded him.
            - May 3, 1939: Formed the Forward Bloc within Congress to rally radical elements, advocating mass action.
            - Faced disciplinary action after calling an All India Protest against an AICC resolution diluting anti-British resolve:
                - Removed as Bengal Provincial Congress Committee president.
                - Banned from Congress elective offices for three years (1939–1942).

    - Congress and World War II: From Resignation to Cripps Mission
        - Overview
            - The outbreak of World War II in September 1939 tested the Indian National Congress’s strategies, revealing internal divisions and escalating demands for independence. 
            - From ministry resignations to the Cripps Mission, this period marked a shift from conditional cooperation to renewed agitation, culminating in the prelude to Quit India.
        - Congress Ministries Resign and Initial War Response (October–November 1939)
            - Context
                - Britain’s unilateral declaration of India as a belligerent (hostile and aggressive) in World War II without consulting Indian leaders sparked outrage, prompting Congress to reassess its position.
            - Congress Demands and Actions
                - October 23, 1939: The Congress Working Committee (CWC) met in Wardha, condemning Britain’s decision and demanding consultation and self-determination.
                - Resignation of Ministries: By November 10, Congress-led provincial governments (e.g., Bombay, Madras) resigned, protesting lack of autonomy.
                - Gandhi’s Stance: Welcomed resignations as a “bitter pill” to purify Congress’s anti-imperialist resolve.
            - Divergent Views Within Congress
                - Support for Allies: Gandhi and C. Rajagopalachari sympathized with Britain’s anti-fascist fight but tied support to Indian freedom.
                - Opposition to War: Nehru opposed participation without independence; socialists saw it as an imperialist conflict.
            - Government Response
                - Viceroy Linlithgow’s White Paper (October 17, 1939):
                    - Framed war aims vaguely as resisting aggression, avoiding democratic commitments for India.
                    - Offered post-war Dominion Status and more Indian members in the Viceroy’s Executive Council.
                    - Proposed a consultative committee instead of a national government, reinforcing colonial control.
                - CWC Reaction: Dismissed it as a rehash of colonial policies, opting out of war support and solidifying resignations.
            - Muslim League Reaction
                - Jinnah supported Britain, demanding recognition as the sole Muslim voice and veto power over constitutional changes, celebrating resignations as a “Day of Deliverance” on December 22, 1939.
        - Congress Articulates War Conditions (September–October 1939)
            - Congress Demands via CWC Resolution
                - Argued India couldn’t fight for democracy abroad while denied it at home.
                - Sought an end to colonial rule and full democracy in India.
                - Specific Conditions:
                    - Britain to clarify war goals and their application to India’s democratization.
                    - Post-war constituent assembly to frame India’s constitution.
                    - Immediate establishment of a responsible central government.
            - Government Response
                - Linlithgow’s Rejection: Deemed significant power transfers unsuitable during wartime, leveraging Muslim League and princely state support to counter Congress.
                - Outcome: Strengthened Congress’s resolve to withhold war cooperation, deepening the standoff.
        - Debate on Mass Struggle (Late 1939–Early 1940)
            - Internal Deliberations
                - Arguments Against Immediate Action:
                    - Allies’ just cause warranted restraint to avoid embarrassment during the war.
                    - Risk of communal violence disrupting Hindu-Muslim unity.
                    - Congress’s organizational weaknesses—disarray and lack of discipline post-resignations—hindered mass protests.
                - Radical Push: Socialists and others pressed for immediate resistance, viewing the war as an imperialist opportunity.
        - Ramgarh Congress Session (March 19–20, 1940)
            - Context
                - Presided by Maulana Abul Kalam Azad (editor of *Al-Hilal*), amid growing war pressures and internal debates.
            - Congress Demands and Resolutions
                - Nehru reiterated complete independence as a prerequisite for support, rejecting Dominion Status within the imperial structure.
                - Declared sovereignty must rest with India’s people, not the Crown.
                - Authorized Gandhi to launch Civil Disobedience Movement (CDM) when deemed fit, signaling readiness for struggle.
            - Government Response
                - No immediate British counteroffer; Linlithgow maintained a hardline stance, ignoring Congress’s escalating demands.
        - August Offer (August 8, 1940)
            - Context
                - After the fall of Belgium, Holland, and France (May–June 1940), Britain sought Indian support; Congress proposed an interim government, but Linlithgow issued his own terms.
            - British Proposal (Linlithgow’s Offer)
                - Dominion Status post-war, with a largely Indian constituent assembly.
                - Expanded Viceroy’s Executive Council with majority Indian members from major parties.
                - Right to frame India’s constitution post-war, subject to minority consent.
            - Indian Responses
                - Congress Rejection: Criticized delayed independence and lack of immediate power transfer.
                - Muslim League Support: Welcomed minority veto but insisted on partition, foreshadowing Pakistan.
        - Individual Satyagraha (October 1940–December 1941)
            - Context
                - Launched as a limited protest amid stalled negotiations and Japan’s rising threat in Asia.
            - Congress Objectives and Actions
                - Demonstrated nationalist resolve, countering perceptions of weakness.
                - Protested India’s forced war involvement.
                - Demanded free speech via anti-war declarations (“Delhi Chalo Andolan”).
                - Execution: Began October 17, 1940, with Vinoba Bhave as the first satyagrahi; Nehru followed (arrested October 31); 25,000 arrested by May 1941.
            - Government Response
                - Mass arrests and suppression of anti-war activities, signaling intolerance for dissent.
            - Congress Dilemma and Shift
                - Japan’s aggression (e.g., Pearl Harbor, December 1941) heightened urgency; CWC offered cooperation if full independence was assured post-war and power transferred immediately.
                - Gandhi named Nehru his successor, solidifying leadership for future struggles.
        - Cripps Mission (March–April 1942)
            - Context
                - Japan’s advance (e.g., Singapore’s fall, February 1942) prompted Britain to send Sir Stafford Cripps, arriving March 23, to secure Indian war support.
            - British Proposal (Cripps’ Offer)
                - Dominion Status post-war, with an elected Indian constituent assembly.
                - Provinces could join or secede from the Indian Union, forming separate entities.
                - Defence and communications under British control during the war; post-war, India could retain British forces.
                - Minority safeguards via a commission; secession option if rights were unprotected.
                - Princely states to join voluntarily or form a separate union.
            - Indian Responses
                - Congress Rejection:
                    - Opposed Dominion Status over full independence.
                    - Criticized princely state nominees (not elected), provincial secession, and lack of immediate power transfer (Governor-General’s supremacy retained).
                - Muslim League: Rejected a single union, arguing it denied Muslim self-determination and Pakistan.
                - Hindu Mahasabha: Opposed secession as fragmenting India.
                - Depressed Classes: Feared partition would expose them to upper-caste dominance.
                - Sikhs: Objected to losing Punjab in potential Muslim-majority secession.
            - Assessment and Outcome
                - Rigid “take it or leave it” stance and vague accession process alienated all parties.
                - Gandhi called it a “post-dated cheque”; Nehru criticized its autocratic retention, paving the way for Quit India (August 1942).


- Indian National Movement (1942 - 1947) - Final phase
    - Quit India Movement (August 1942)
        - August Resolution - 'Quit India' “Bharat Chodo Andolan”
            - July 1942 - CWC meeting Wardha - Resolution giving Gandhi the command to launch mass movement - 8 August 1942 Resolution formally ratified.
        - Directives given to Various Sections of Society
            - Government Servants: - Do not resign but declare allegiance to Congress.
            - Soldiers: Stay in the Army but refrain from firing on compatriots.
            - Students: If confident, leave studies to join the movement.
            - Peasants: If zamindars are anti-government, pay mutually agreed rent & If zamindars are pro-government, do not pay rent.
            - Princes: Support the masses and acknowledge the sovereignty of the people.
            - People in Princely States: Support rulers only if they are anti-government & Declare allegiance to being part of the Indian nation.
        - Who All Opposed
            - Muslim League
            - Communist Party of India
            - Hindu Mahasabha
        - Course of Movement
            - In a single Sweep, (9 August 1942) all the Top Leaders of Congress were arrested and taken to unknown locations.
            - Declared Congress organisations unlawful, Public meeting banned under Defense of India Act, 1919.
            - Led to emergence of Local Level Leaders and unregulated mass movements - Leading to violent incidents.
                - Parallel Governments (September 1942)
                    - Balliya Sarkar - Chittu Pandey - Ballia (UP) - Low Budget Gandhi Marriages
                    - Tamluk Sarkar = Satish C. Samanta - Tamluk (Bengal) - Vidyut Vahini (Armed Militia)
                    - Satara Sarkar - Nana Patil = Satara (MH) - Nyayadan Mandals (People's Court) & Toofan Senas (Youth Militia)
            - Emergence of Underground Activities
                - Disrupting Communications
                - Underground Radio Station - Bombay - Usha Mehta (Movie - Ae Watan Mera Watan)
        - Govt Response to Movement
            - Utilised Large Military Force - initially stationed to confront Japanese.
            - Wartime censorship.
            - In Feb 1943, Gandhi took fast unto death - Govt, anticipated Gandhi's death and made arrangements for his funeral.
            - Bengal Famine of 1943 - One of the reasons for decline of movement initially, later emerged as a strong reason for independence
                - Bengal Famine of 1943
                    - The Bengal Famine of 1943 was one of the deadliest humanitarian disasters in modern Indian history, leading to the deaths of an estimated 2-3 million people due to starvation, malnutrition, and disease.
                - Causes of the Famine
                    - British War Policies & Economic Exploitation
                        - The famine occurred during World War II, when British policies prioritized war efforts over Indian welfare.
                        - "Denial Policy" (1942): To prevent a Japanese invasion, the British destroyed boats and food stocks in coastal Bengal, disrupting local economies.
                        - Rice export bans were lifted for British war allies, worsening domestic shortages.
                    - Hoarding and Inflation
                        - Wartime policies created hyperinflation, making food unaffordable.
                        - Wealthy traders and speculators hoarded rice, creating an artificial scarcity.
                    - British Apathy and Churchill's Indifference
                        - Winston Churchill refused to send food aid, blaming Indians for "breeding like rabbits."
                        - When Indian officials requested 1 million tons of grain, Britain sent only 50,000 tons, prioritizing European stockpiles.
                    - Cyclones and Crop Failures
                        - A cyclone in October 1942 and fungal diseases reduced the rice yield in Bengal, worsening the crisis.
                - Impact of the Famine
                    - Deaths and Suffering
                        - 2-3 million people died due to starvation, disease, and malnutrition.
                        - Malaria and cholera outbreaks worsened the death toll.
                    - Political Fallout
                        - The famine exposed British colonial neglect, increasing nationalist sentiments.
                        - Subhas Chandra Bose used it as propaganda to rally Indians against British rule.
                        - The Indian National Congress and Mahatma Gandhi condemned the British, calling for immediate independence.
                    - Economic Collapse
                        - Rural Bengal's economy collapsed, pushing millions into poverty.
                        - The famine devastated farmers, laborers, and fishermen, many of whom migrated or died.
        - Significance of Movement
            - Unprecedented Mass Participation with Grassroot leadership
            - Use of Violence

    - Indian National Army (1942 - 1945)
        - Indian Independence League (IIL)
            - In March 1942, Rash Behari Bose, a revolutionary leader in Japan since 1915, organized a meeting in Tokyo with other Indian leaders to form the IIL.
            - In June 1942, during a meeting in Bangkok, Rash Behari Bose was appointed as the President of the IIL.
            - A resolution was passed to establish the Indian National Army (INA), also known as the Azad Hind Fauz.
            - It was decided that Subhas Chandra Bose would lead the movement in the future.
        - Formation of the INA
            - INA enlisted volunteers from Indians overseas and ex-British Indian soldiers in Southeast Asia.
            - Captain Mohan Singh was made the Commander-in-Chief.
            - The INA grew to about 40,000 members, preparing to confront British forces in India.
            - Disagreements between Captain Mohan Singh and the Japanese led to the transfer of INA leadership to Subhas Chandra Bose.
        - INA under Subhas Chandra Bose
            - In July 1943, Rash Behari Bose handed over the leadership to Subhas Chandra Bose.
            - Subhas Bose established the Arzi Hukumat-e-Azad Hind (Provisional Government of Free India) in Singapore on October 21, 1943.
            - The Provisional Government declared war on Britain and the USA and was recognized by the Axis powers.
            - In Dec 1943, Japanese handed over Andaman and Nicobar Island to Bose - Renaming Shaheed and Swaraj Dweep.
            - INA HQ's shifted from Singapore to Rangoon in January 1944 and army prepared to march towards Delhi (Dilli Chalo)
                - At this time regiment of INA were named after Gandhi, Nehru, Azar, Bose, Rani Jhansi (Captain Lakshmi Sehgal)
                - INA Army was arrested by Allied Forces in 1945 meanwhile Subash Bose died in an air crash on August 18, 1945 in Taipei, Taiwan

    - The Story Continues - CR (C. Rajagopalachari) Formula (1944)
        - Following Gandhi's release from Jail on May 5, 1944 - He tried to revive Congress and also initiated discussions with Muslim League in July 1944.
        - C. R. Formula (1944)
            - Proposed by: C. Rajagopalachari
            - Objective: To resolve the deadlock between the Indian National Congress and the Muslim League over the demand for Pakistan and to facilitate cooperation between the two parties for achieving Indian independence.
            - Main Points
                - The Muslim League was to cooperate with the Congress in forming a Provisional Interim Government for a transitional period and in return League's demand for Pakistan was recognized, but only through a democratic process involving a plebiscite (vote or referendum).
                - All parties would be allowed to express their views on partition before the plebiscite.
                - After World War II, a plebiscite would decide whether Muslim-majority areas in the North-West and North-East would:
                    - Form a separate sovereign state (Pakistan), or Remain a part of India.
                - If separation occurred, agreements would be entered into for essential services such as:
                    - Defence
                    - Communication
                    - Commerce.
        - Reactions to C. R. Formula (1944)
            - Muslim League's Stand
                - Supported the concept of a two-nation theory.
                - Muhammad Ali Jinnah proposed that
                    - Only Muslims in the North-West and North-East should vote in the plebiscite.
                    - The rest of the Indian population should not be involved in the decision-making process.
            - Congress's Stand
                - Congress was willing to work with the Muslim League to achieve independence.
                - However, Congress rejected the formula as
                    - It accepted the principle of partition.
                    - The League's primary goal was the creation of a separate nation, which Congress opposed.
            - Other Reactions
                - Hindu Leaders
                    - Led by Vir Savarkar, Hindu leaders opposed the formula, as they disagreed with conceding to the demand for Pakistan.
                - Sikh Leaders
                    - Leaders like Master Tara Singh opposed the plan, fearing division of the Sikh community in Punjab, leaving Sikhs on both sides of the dividing line.

    - Desai Liaquat Pact (1945)
        - Context
            - The failure of the Gandhi-Jinnah Talks (1944) left a deadlock between the INC and the Muslim League.
            - Secret talks were held between Bhulabhai Desai, a senior Congress leader, and Liaquat Ali Khan, a prominent leader of the Muslim League, in January 1945.
        - Objective
            - To negotiate an agreement for a future coalition government representing both Hindus and Muslims equally in the independent government of India.
        - Key Points of the Pact
            - Recognition of the Muslim League: - Desai acknowledged the Muslim League as the representative of Muslims.
            - Formation of an Interim Government: An interim government would be formed with equal representation of the Congress and the League.
            - Liaquat's Concessions: Liaquat Ali Khan gave up the demand for a separate Muslim state (Pakistan).
            - Seats for Minorities: The pact proposed 20% reserved seats for minorities in the government.
        - Outcome
            - The Desai-Liaquat Pact was never formally endorsed by either the Congress or the Muslim League.

    - Special Mention - Sapru Committee Report (1945)

    - Wavell Plan (1945)
        - Background
            - By the end of 1944, with the war turning in favor of the Allies, the British realized that
                - The Indian situation, post-Quit India Movement, could not be ignored and prolonged use of force to subdue India indefinitely was impractical
            - Urgency of Finding a Solution - The upcoming 1945 General Elections in England required the Conservatives to urgently address Indian issues
            - The plan was designed to prevent Congress leaders from exploiting the post-war climate of unemployment and economic challenges.
        - Key Features of the Wavell Plan
            - Restructured Executive Council: - The Executive Council was to be restructured with all members, except the Governor-General and Commander-in-Chief, being Indian representatives.
            - Interim Government: - The interim government would function within the framework of the 1935 Government of India Act and would remain answerable to the Central Assembly.
            - Joint List of Nominees
                - Political parties were to collaborate in creating a joint list of nominees for the Executive Council.
                - If consensus was not achieved, separate lists could be submitted.
            - Equal Representation - Equal representation was to be provided to caste Hindus and Muslims in the Executive Council.
            - Governor-General's Powers -The Governor-General would exercise veto power, but only on the advice of his ministers.
            - Future Constitution - Provisions were made to explore negotiations for a new constitution after the war.
        - Simla Conference (June 1945)
            - To implement the Wavell Plan, Lord Wavell organized a conference in Simla
                - Congress was represented by Maulana Azad, while Jinnah represented the Muslim League.
                - Mahatma Gandhi also advised the Viceroy during the talks.
            - Reactions to the Wavell Plan
                - Muslim League's Response
                    - Feared loss of representation if other minority groups (like Sikhs, Christians, and Depressed Classes) aligned with the Congress.
                    - Demanded that all Muslim members in the Executive Council be nominated by the League.
                - Congress's Response
                    - Perceived the plan as an attempt to limit Congress's influence, seeing it as a measure to make Congress solely a caste Hindu party.
                    - Asserted its right to nominate representatives from all communities.
                
    - Political Negotiations and Developments
        - The Release of Congress Leaders (Post-1944)
            - Post-World War II, the British sought to establish a coalition government in India. (Wavell Plan 1945)
            - Congress leaders were released from prison in May 1944 on grounds of ill health.
            - Public enthusiasm for these leaders signaled renewed resolve to fight for independence.
        - Elections in Britain (1945)
            - The Labour Party, sympathetic to India, won the July 1945 elections.
            - Clement Attlee became Prime Minister, replacing Winston Churchill.
            - The Labour Party manifesto highlighted promoting India's self-government within the Commonwealth.
            - In August 1945, elections for India's central and provincial legislatures were announced.
            - Viceroy Wavell indicated in September that following the election, Government would intend to
                - Hold Negotiations for establishing a Constitution making body.
                - Form an Executive Council with support from the major political parties.
        - Election in India (1945 & 1946)
            - Muslim League Manifesto
                - Asserted Muslims of India are One Nation.
                - Emphasized Pakistan as the solution to India's constitutional problem.
            - Congress Manifesto
                - Advocated for Indian unity and opposed partition.
                - Raised public issues like Police excesses during QIM and INA Trials.
            - Election Results (1946): The Elections to Central Legislative Assembly were held in December 1945 and the provincial elections were held in January 1946.
                - Congress dominated the elections, winning 91.3% of non-Muslim votes and 57/102 seats in the Central Legislative Assembly.
                - Muslim League secured 86.6% of Muslim votes, winning 30/30 Muslim seats in the Assembly and 402/509 Muslim seats in provinces.
                - Congress formed ministries in 8 provinces (Majority everywhere except Sindh, Punjab and Bengal), while the Muslim League led in Sindh and Bengal.
                    - In Punjab, Congress, Union Party and Akalis formed a coalition Government.
        - Popular Uprising - INA Trials (1945-46)
            - Importance and Impact of INA Trials
                - Widespread Political Support
                    - All major political parties, including Congress, Akalis, Communist Party of India, Hindu Mahasabha, Justice Party, Muslim League, RSS, and Unionist Party.
                - Broadened Appeal
