                    - Attracted new social groups like government officials, armed forces members, and loyalists who were previously outside the nationalist movement.
                - Geographic Spread of Support
                    - Support extended beyond major cities like Delhi, Bombay, Calcutta, and Madras to remote regions such as Assam, Balochistan, and Coorg.
                - Public Symbolism and Outrage
                    - Held publicly at the Red Fort, the trials symbolized British oppression and sparked mass public outrage.
                - Prominent Figures
                    - Key INA leaders, Shah Nawaz Khan, Prem Sehgal, and Gurbaksh Dhillon, were charged with treason (betrayel one's country).
            - INA Defence Committee and Trial Outcome
                - Formation of INA Defence Committee (1945)
                    - The INC established the INA Defence Committee, including prominent lawyers like
                        - Jawaharlal Nehru, Bhulabhai Desai, Asaf Ali, and Tej Bahadur Sapru.
                - Outcome of the Trial
                    - INA members were convicted of conducting war but were not executed.
                    - They were terminated from service with no long-term punishment.
        - Popular Uprising - RIN Mutiny
            - Growth of the Royal Indian Navy
                - The Royal Indian Navy expanded rapidly during World War II, becoming 10 times larger between 1939 and 1945.
            - Surge in Nationalism
                - A growing sense of nationalism among Indian sailors played a crucial role in shaping the mutiny.
            - Contributing Factors
                - Disparities in pay and working conditions.
                - Racial discrimination and unequal treatment by British officers.
                - Poor living conditions and inadequate food supplies.
                - Resentment over being used to suppress freedom movements in other colonies.
                - Influence of the INA trials and broader anti-colonial sentiment.
            - Mutiny Events
                - Started on December 1, 1945 at HMIS Talwar in Bombay. (Training Facility for Royal Indian Navy)
                - Formation of Naval Central Strike Committee formed with Lt. M.S. Khan (President) and officer Madan Singh (Vice President). This committee had representatives from all Bombay ships and barracks and renamed RIN to Indian National Navy.
                - The Committee presented demands like
                    - Release of all political prisoners including that of INA Trials.
                    - Equal Pay and Better food quality.
                    - End of British Rule in India.
                - Expanded to include 20,000 ratings (enlisted sailors or non-commissioned personnel in the navy) and several naval stations across India.
                - Ratings raised Congress, Muslim League, and Communist Party flags, symbolizing unity.
            - Political Response
                - Clement Attlee called for a forceful resolution.
                - Congress and Muslim League opposed the mutiny, fearing violence, Gandhi too opposed the unplanned revolt.
                - Communist Party of India fully supported the naval ratings and mobilised workers in Bombay by shutting down textile mills.
            - End of Mutiny & Significance
                - Mutiny was eventually quelled after negotiations, Principal demands were met and under pressure from Congress Naval Central Strike Committee was forced to surrender.
                    - Following the RIN Mutiny, all participating Navy personnels were dismissed and many faced court martials. However in 1972, GOI recognised their contribution and granted them freedom fighter's pensions.
                - The timing of Cabinet Missions announcement, just a day after the Mutiny starts underscores its influence on British Policies towards India.
        - Cabinet Mission (1946)
            - The Labour Government, led by Clement Attlee, sent the Cabinet Mission to India to explore solutions to Indian problems and resolve constitutional deadlock - The mission comprised three British officials
                - Lord Pethick-Lawrence (Secretary of State for India).
                - Sir Stafford Cripps (President of the Board of Trade and a supporter of Indian self-determination).
                - A.V. Alexander (First Lord of the Admiralty).
            - Objectives of the Cabinet Mission
                - Resolve Constitutional Issues
                    - Design a framework for India's constitution to address competing demands of Congress and the Muslim League.
                - Avoid Partition
                    - Prevent the division of India into separate sovereign states (India and Pakistan).
                - Negotiate Interim Government
                    - Facilitate the formation of an interim government to oversee the transition.
            - After initial dialogue, Mission proposed its plan for composition of new Government on 16 May 1946.
            - Mission came with short term (Formation of Interim Government) and long term proposals (Federal and Constituent Assembly proposals) to ensure a peaceful transition while addressing the complex political and communal issues in India
                - Interim Government
                    - An Interim Government was to be established with representatives from major political parties to manage the transition.
                - Rejection of a Separate Pakistan
                    - Geographical impracticality of dividing provinces like Bengal and Punjab.
                    - Risk of economic and administrative challenges in divided territories.
                - Constituent Assembly
                    - It was to be elected by provincial assemblies by proportional representation
                    - Recommended the formation of a 389-member Constituent Assembly
                        - 292 members from British provinces.
                        - 93 members from princely states.
                        - 4 members from chief commissioners' provinces.
                - Federal Structure
                    - Proposed a three-tier government system
                        - Union Government: Control over foreign affairs, defense, and communications.
                            - Communal Questions in the Central Legislature were to be decided by simple majority in both communities present and voting.
                        - Provincial Governments: Autonomy in other areas and also Residual Powers.
                        - Groups of Provinces
                            - Section A - Hindu Majority Provinces (Madras, Bombay, United Provinces, Bihar and Orissa)
                            - Section B - Punjab, NWFP (North-West Frontier Provinc), Sindh (Muslim majority provinces of North West)
                            - Section C - Bengal and Assam (Muslim dominated provinces of North East)
                    - After the first General Elections, a province was to be free to come out of a group and also eligible for reconsideration.
                    - Princely States were no longer to be under the paramountcy of the British Govt. They would be free to enter into an agreement with successor governments of the British Govt.
            - Reaction to the Cabinet Mission
                - Congress's Response
                    - Accepted the idea of a Constituent Assembly and went ahead to form the Constituent Assembly (15 June)
                    - Rejected the groupings of provinces, (10 July) as it contradicted their vision of a unified India. (Initially, thought that grouping was optional for provinces and free to join or not)
                - Muslim League's Response
                    - Muslim League took the grouping of provinces to be compulsory and thus leading realization of Pakistan indirectly.
                    - Initially accepted (June 6) the plan but later withdrew, accusing Congress of undermining the grouping principle (29 July) and the right of Muslim League only to nominate Muslim candidates (Zakir Hussain from Congress)
                    - Called for Direct Action Day (August 16, 1946) to press for the creation of Pakistan after Congress was invited by Wavell to form Interim Govt at center sidelining Muslim League.
                - Princely States' Response
                    - The princely states were reluctant to join the proposed federal structure, fearing loss of autonomy.
            - Formation of Interim Government with Congress Alone
                - Invitation to Congress
                    - On 12 August 1946, the Viceroy invited Jawaharlal Nehru to form the Interim Government as Prime Minister.
                    - A 12-member cabinet was formed, representing diverse sections of society
                        - 5 Hindus
                        - 3 Muslims
                        - 1 each from Scheduled Castes, Sikhs, Christians, and Parsis.
                - Provincial Governments
                    - At the provincial level, Congress-led governments were formed in most provinces, including
                        - North-West Frontier Province (NWFP).
                        - Punjab: Coalition with Akali Dal and Unionist Muslim League.
                        - Bengal and Sindh: Muslim League formed governments.
                - Constituent Assembly's Role
                    - The Constituent Assembly was instructed to commence work on drafting a new constitution for India.
                - Muslim League's Opposition
                    - Muhammad Ali Jinnah condemned the Interim Government and pledged to continue the fight for Pakistan.
                    - This led to widespread unrest and violence in Punjab, Delhi, Bombay, and other regions.
                - Wavell's Appeal
                    - In light of communal tensions, Viceroy Wavell urged Nehru to include the Muslim League in the Interim Government, hoping it would restore peace.
                    - Despite this, the Muslim League did not abandon its demand for Pakistan and direct action to achieve it.
                - League's Inclusion
                    - On 26 October 1946, the Muslim League joined the Interim Government, with Liaqat Ali Khan appointed as Finance Minister. The League aimed to disrupt the functioning of the government, using its position to block key decisions.
                    - These tactics convinced Congress leaders of the futility (pointlessness) of the Interim Government.
                - Breakdown of the Interim Government
                    - Mountbatten's Arrival: With the arrival of the new Viceroy, Lord Mountbatten, in early 1947, Congress leaders expressed that the coalition was unworkable.
                    - Acceptance of Partition: The failure of the Interim Government, coupled with escalating communal tensions, led to the acceptance of the idea of Partition, paving the way for the division of India.
        - Constituent Assembly: Formation and Election
            - The Cabinet Mission aimed to make immediate arrangements for Indians to decide the future constitution of India, resolving political conflicts. The assembly was constituted under the Government of India Act, 1935, using restricted adult franchise.
            - Composition and Electoral College
                - Composition of Assembly
                    - The Constituent Assembly had 389 seats
                        - 296 seats for British India provinces (e.g., Bengal, Bihar, Odisha, etc.).
                        - 93 seats for princely states.
                - Division of Seats
                    - Seats were distributed based on population: (Roughly one seat per million people)
                    - Seats in British provinces were also divided among: Muslims, Sikhs, and General communities based on their population.
                - Election Process
                    - Representatives of each community were elected by members of that community in the provincial legislative assembly.
                    - Voting followed the method of proportional representation.
                - Princely States
                    - Representatives were nominated by the heads of princely states.
                - First Session (9 December 1946)
                    - Only 207 of the 296 members attended the inaugural session.
                    - Members from the Muslim League boycotted the session due to disagreements over partition.
                    - J.B. Kripalani invited Dr. Sachchidanand Sinha to act as the temporary chairman due to his seniority.
                - Election of Permanent Chairman (10 December 1946)
                    - Dr. Rajendra Prasad was elected as the permanent chairman of the Constituent Assembly.
                - Objective Resolution (13 December 1946)
                    - Jawaharlal Nehru proposed the Objective Resolution, which later formed the basis of the Preamble to the Indian Constitution.
                - Dual Role
                    - Acted as the Constituent Assembly, framing the Constitution.
                    - Functioned as the Legislative Assembly, governing and making laws.
                - Timeframe
                    - The Assembly held 11 sessions over 2 years and 11 months for a total of 166 days.
                    - It completed the Constitution of India in 1950.
        - Partition and Freedom
            - Muslim League's Direct Action Day and Aftermath
                - Direct Action Day (16 August 1946)
                    - Declared by the Muslim League to demand Pakistan.
                    - Resulted in communal riots, creating a crisis-like situation across India.
                - League's Position
                    - Despite Viceroy Wavell's efforts, the League
                        - Refused to cooperate in the Interim Government.
                        - Declined to participate in the Constituent Assembly.
                - Escalation
                    - On 5 February 1947, Congress requested the resignation of League members from the Interim Government.
                    - Prime Minister Clement Attlee acknowledged the situation in the British Parliament on 20 February 1947, marking an irreversible breakdown of the Interim Government.
            - Attlee's Declaration (20 February 1947)
                - Key Points
                    - British Withdrawal Date: Scheduled for 30 June 1948, regardless of whether a constitution was finalized.
                    - If the Constituent Assembly lacked full representation by June 1948, power would be transferred to multiple central governments. (If Provinces with Muslim majority did not join)
                    - British powers over princely states would cease but would not transfer to any subsequent Indian government.
                    - Lord Louis Mountbatten was appointed to replace Wavell as Viceroy in March 1947.
                - Reactions
                    - Congress: Accepted the idea of transferring power to multiple central governments.
                    - Muslim League: Jinnah became resolute in his demand for Pakistan and launched
                        - Efforts to overthrow non-League governments in Punjab, Assam, and NWFP (North-West Frontier Provinc) using violence.
                        - A civil disobedience movement in Punjab, causing the collapse of Khizr Hayat Khan's coalition ministry.
            - Mountbatten Plan (June 1947)
                - Initial Proposals
                    - Suggested dividing provinces into independent successor states under the Balkan/Dickie Bird Plan.
                    - This plan allowed provinces like Punjab and Bengal to choose independence, Pakistan, or India through votes.
                    - Jawaharlal Nehru strongly opposed the Balkan Plan, leading to its abandonment.
                - Key Features of Mountbatten Plan
                    - Partition: India would be divided into two dominions—India and Pakistan with Dominion Status and their respective Constituent Assemblies.
                    - Partition of Punjab and Bengal - Members of Legislative Assemblies of Punjab and Bengal should meet separately to vote for partition, if both sections of each of these assemblies voted for partition, then that province would be partitioned.
                    - Referendum in Sylhet, NWFP and Voting in Sindh Legislative Assembly to decide their fate.
                    - Boundary Commission under Sir Cyril Radcliffe for demarcating the boundaries of new parts of Punjab and Bengal.
                    - Princely States - British Suzerainty over Princely states will end, they were theoretically independent as per plan. However it was made clear in the long run that had the option to either choose India or Pakistan.
                    - Transfer of Power - British would leave India on 15 August, 1947.
                - Indian Reaction and Result
                    - Nehru, Jinnah and Sardar Baldev Singh representing Sikhs, endorsed the plan on June 4, 1947.
                    - On June 20, Bengal Assembly and on June 23, Punjab assembly both voted in favour of partition.
                    - As per referendum, NWFP and Sylhet went to Pakistan.
                    - On June 26, 1947, at a special session Sindh Assembly also decided to join Pakistan.
                    - The Boundary Commission award was ready by 12 August, 1947 - Ready to divide India.
                    - Thus, Indian Independence Act 1947 was passed by the British Parliament. (4 July 1947)
                        - Indian Independence Act 1947
                            - Declaration of Independence
                                - India and Pakistan were declared independent and sovereign dominions from August 15, 1947, with the partition dividing British India into two sovereign states. The title of the King of England was modified to exclude “Emperor of India.”.
                            - Status of Princely States
                                - British paramountcy over princely states and tribal territories lapsed, granting princely states the autonomy to
                                    - Join India or Pakistan.
                                    - Opt for independence.
                                - 
                            - Governance Changes
                                - The Viceroy's office was abolished, and each dominion was to have a Governor-General appointed by the British Crown on the recommendation of the dominion's government. The Governors-General and provincial governors acted as nominal heads, following ministerial advice.
                                - The Secretary of State for India's office was abolished, transferring duties to the Secretary of State for Commonwealth Affairs, and entitlements for civil servants appointed before August 15, 1947, were continued.
                            - Legislative Functions
                                - Constituent Assemblies of both dominions served as legislative bodies with powers to
                                    - Dissolve the Central Legislative Assembly and Council of State.
                                    - Annul any Act of the British Parliament, including the Independence Act itself.
                                - The Government of India Act, 1935, served as interim constitutional law until new constitutions were adopted.
                            - British Involvement
                                - His Majesty's Government in Britain bore no responsibility for the governance of India or Pakistan after August 15, 1947. British Parliamentary Acts could not extend to the dominions without approval by the dominion's legislature.
                            - Message by Kawal Sir - Hello Students, The content given under below topics form as the most important area of Modern History from where questions are being asked in the last few Years
                            

- British Administrative System
    - Overview
        - By the late 18th century, the East India Company shifted from a trading entity to a political authority in India. To sustain their rule, the British developed a robust administrative system built on three key pillars: Judicial System, Civil Services, and Army.
        - Aims of the British Administration
            - Efficiently implement British policies across India.
            - Secure revenue collection and maintain law and order to stabilize their control.
            - Profoundly transform India's socio-economic landscape to align with British interests.
        - Purpose of the Administrative Framework
            - Establish British supremacy over Indian territories.
            - Exploit Indian resources to fuel imperial ambitions.

        - British Methods of Expansion
            - Direct Conquest
                - The British expanded their control in India through direct military conquests, annexing key regions such as Bengal, Mysore, the Maratha territories, Sindh, and Punjab. These victories established their dominance over significant parts of the subcontinent.
            - Ring Fence Policy
                - Implemented by Warren Hastings, this doctrine aimed to protect British territories by securing their neighboring frontiers. It involved creating a buffer zone around British holdings to safeguard their empire from external threats.
            - Subsidiary Alliance
                - Perfected by Lord Wellesley, this system involved Indian rulers entering treaties with the British East India Company. In exchange for protection from external enemies and internal rebellions, these rulers accepted British oversight, effectively ceding much of their autonomy. (I hope you understand this concept in detail.)
            - Doctrine of Lapse
                - Introduced by Lord Dalhousie, this policy targeted princely states under British subsidiary alliance or suzerainty. If a ruler was deemed unfit to govern by the British or died without a male heir, the state would lose its princely status and be annexed into British India, expanding British territorial control.

    - British India - Judicial System
        - Pre-Colonial Judicial System
            - Before British rule, India's judicial system lacked uniformity and organization. Hindu disputes were settled by caste elders, village panchayats, or zamindars, while Muslim cases were overseen by Qazis with religious expertise.
        - Judicial Reforms Under Warren Hastings (1772-1785) (For Understanding)
            - Goals
                - Ensure efficient resolution of disputes involving tenants, farmers, and government officers.
                - Address complex inheritance issues, leading to the creation of "A Code of Gentoo Laws or Ordination of Pundits" in 1776.
            - Reforms
                - Established Mofussil Diwani Adalat for civil cases like property disputes or contracts.
                - Created Faujdari Adalat to handle criminal cases such as theft or assault.
                - Set up higher appellate courts in Calcutta: Sadar Diwani Adalat for civil appeals and Sadar Nizamat Adalat for criminal appeals.
                - Islamic authorities, Qazis and Muftis, presided (be in the position of authority in a meeting or other gathering) over criminal courts under the Governor-General's supervision.
            - Regulating Act of 1773
                - Introduced the Supreme Court in Calcutta with jurisdiction over both original and appellate cases.
        - Judicial Reforms Under Lord Cornwallis (1786-1793)
            - Judicial Plan of 1787
                - Merged tax and judicial systems, with the Collector presiding over revenue disputes in Maal Adalats and civil disputes in District Diwani Adalats.
            - Judicial Plan of 1790
                - Transferred criminal justice administration from Muslim Law Officers to Company officers, abolishing District Faujdari Courts and replacing them with Circuit Courts (traveling courts). Sadar Nizamat Adalat was retained for criminal appeals.
            - Judicial Plan of 1793
                - Separated revenue and judicial administration clearly: Maal Adalats were abolished, revenue issues were handled by Collectors, and District Judges oversaw District Diwani Adalats.
            - 

    - British India - Civil Services
        - Overview
            - The Indian Civil Services (ICS) were established to maintain law and order, facilitate governance, and manage trade in British India, evolving over time to meet administrative demands.
        - Evolution of Civil Services
            - Formative Stage (1675-1765)
                - The Covenanted Civil Service emerged, separating commercial agents from military and naval officers. Members managed coastal trade stations under a signed "covenant," earning the title "covenanted civil servants."
            - Transition to Administrative Service (1765-1853)
                - Regulating Act of 1773: 
                    - Separated civil and commercial functions, banning private trade and bribery among civil servants.
                - Pitt's India Act of 1784: 
                    - Set recruitment age limits, empowered the Crown to remove corrupt officials, and under Lord Cornwallis (Father of Civil Services in India) introduced two divisions: Covenanted Service (higher posts for Europeans) and Uncovenanted Service (lower posts open to Indians).
                - Charter Act of 1793: 
                    - Filled vacancies from existing company civil servants.
                - Reforms Under Lord Wellesley (1800): 
                    - Founded Fort William College in 1800 to train civil servants in Oriental culture, law, and administration, replaced by Haileybury Institution in England in 1806 (abolished in 1857).
                - Charter Act of 1833: 
                    - Proposed competitive exams for recruitment, introducing open competition (implemented in 1853).
                - Charter Act of 1853: 
                    - Allowed Indians into the Covenanted Service via open competition. The Macaulay Committee of 1854 established the Civil Service Commission in London in 1855 to oversee examinations.
            - Imperial Civil Services (1858-1917)
                - Indian Civil Service Act of 1861: 
                    - Made any Indian or European resident in India for seven years eligible to qualify.
                - Statutory Civil Service (1879): 
                    - Reserved 20% of covenanted posts for Indians from prominent families, nominated by local governments, following 1870 British Parliament approval of Indian appointments.
                - Major Commissions and Reforms
                    - Aitchison Commission (1886): 
                        - Proposed abolishing "covenanted civil service," recommending classification into Imperial, Provincial, and Subordinate Services for broader Indian inclusion.
                    - Islington Commission (1912): 
                        - Suggested reserving 25% of posts for Indians, categorizing services into Class I and Class II under the Government of India.
                    - 1919 Act: 
                        - Divided services into All India, Central, and Provincial categories, proposing a Public Service Commission (exams began in India from 1922). The Lee Commission (1923) further refined this structure.
                    - 1935 Act: 
                        - Introduced provincial autonomy, retaining only Indian Civil Service, Indian Police Service, and Indian Medical Service as All India Services, while others became provincial. Mandated Public Service Commissions for provinces and the federation.

    - British India - Army
        - Overview
            - The Royal Indian Army was vital for maintaining and expanding the British Empire in India, primarily composed of local soldiers led by British officers.
        - Structure and Reforms
            - Until April 1895, armies in the Bengal, Bombay, and Madras Presidencies operated as separate Presidency Armies. They were unified into a single Indian Army in 1895.
            - For administrative convenience, the army was divided into four commands: Punjab, Bengal, Madras, and Bombay.
            - In 1902, Commander-in-Chief Lord Kitchener's reforms reorganized the Indian Army into two commands—North and South. Each division had three brigades: two Indian battalions and one English battalion. Efficiency was enhanced through the rigorous "Kitchener Test" applied to every battalion.
        - Related Committees
            - Peel Commission (1859)
            - Eden Commission (1879)
            - Rawlinson Committee (1923)
            - Skeen Committee (1925) (Indian Sandhurst Committee)

    - British India - Police
        - Law Enforcement Under the Mughals
            - In rural areas, faujdars upheld law and order, while in urban centers, kotwals managed enforcement.
            - Under Bengal's dual administration, zamindars maintained law and order, supported by staff like thanedars. In 1770, the faujdar institution was abolished, altering this structure.
        - Police System During British Rule
            - Warren Hastings' Reforms (1774)
                - Reestablished faujdars to maintain law and order, directing zamindars to suppress crimes like dacoity (armed robbery), violence, and regional disorder.
            - 1775
                - Set up faujdar thanas (major police stations) in significant towns and regions, supported by minor stations for decentralized enforcement.
            - Major Objectives of British Police
                - Maintain law and order across British territories, suppress rebellions and uprisings for stability, and prevent and control crimes like loot, theft, robbery, and dacoity.
            - Lord Cornwallis' Reforms (1791)
                - Modernized the traditional Indian policing system to suit British needs, relieving zamindars of policing duties to curb abuses, dividing districts into thanas (police circles) under darogas (Indian officers), and appointing a Superintendent of Police (SP) to head district policing.
            - Lord William Bentinck's Reforms (1828-1835)
                - Abolished the Superintendent of Police position, making the Collector/Magistrate the head of the district police force, with a Commissioner acting as SP in each division. This poorly organized system overburdened the Collector/Magistrate, reducing efficiency.
            - Indian Police Act (1861)
                - Based on the 1860 Police Commission's recommendations, it created a police force in every state and union territory, setting guidelines for recruitment, training, and deployment. It defined police powers for maintaining order, preventing and detecting crimes, and enforcing British laws, but failed to establish an All-India Police Service, leaving regional disparities.
            - Indian Police Commission Recommendations (1902)
                - Led by Andrew Fraser, it recommended establishing a Criminal Investigation Department (CID) in provinces and a Central Intelligence Bureau at the center, separating law enforcement from investigation duties, and creating two service branches: an Imperial Service Branch (senior posts from England) and a Provincial Service Branch (lower ranks from India).


- Economic Impact of British Rule
    - Village Conditions
        - Demographics
            - In the mid-18th century, approximately 90% of India's population lived in villages.
        - Primary Occupation
            - Agriculture dominated village life, with landowners and tenants growing crops primarily for self-consumption. Other occupations included artisans, village officials, and menial laborers.
        - Social Structure
            - Traditional customs governed rent, wages, and prices within villages. Sir Charles Metcalfe called them “Little Republics,” emphasizing their autonomy and self-sufficiency, as they produced nearly everything their inhabitants needed.

    - Urban Conditions
        - Demographics
            - Urban dwellers made up about 10% of the population.
        - Types of Urban Centers
            - Religious Centers: Towns like Gaya and Banaras thrived as prominent pilgrimage sites.
            - Administrative Capitals: Cities such as Delhi, Agra, and Lucknow served as provincial capitals.
            - Commercial Hubs: Surat and Hubli emerged as key trading and port centers.

    - Agriculture
        - Characteristics
            - Farmers grew crops mainly for sustenance, sharing surplus with village artisans. Agricultural growth was constrained by dependence on monsoons.
        - Crops
            - Food crops included rice, wheat, bajra, jowar, and minor cereals. Commercial crops comprised jute, raw cotton, and groundnut.
        - Land Revenue System
            - Land revenue was a major income source for the state. Zamindars collected taxes and managed local judicial and police duties. The revenue-sharing system split crops between peasants and the state, with the state typically taking one-third.

    - Trade in Pre-British India
        - Nature of Trade
            - India had extensive domestic and international trade, linked to Asia and Europe through land and maritime routes like the Silk Road.
        - Exported Items
            - Cotton textiles, raw silk, indigo, opium, rice, wheat, sugar, pepper, spices, precious stones, and drugs.
        - Imported Items
            - Pearls, wool, dried fruits, and rosewater from Persia and the Gulf; coffee and gold from Arabia; tea and sugar from China; metals and paper from Europe.
        - Key Ports
            - Surat, Cambay, Calicut, and Masulipatnam were vital trade centers.
        - Trade Balance
            - India enjoyed a favorable trade balance, exporting more than it imported.
        - Local Business Communities
            - Hindu, Jain, and Bohra merchants in Gujarat; Khatris and Lohanis in Punjab; Marwari Banias in Rajasthan.

    - Important Keywords
        - Aurang: 
            - A permanent market or warehouse for storing and trading goods, used during Mughal and British rule.
        - Banian: 
            - Indian intermediaries or brokers who aided European traders in trade and finance.
        - Mirasidar: 
            - A hereditary landowner in South India, primarily under the Ryotwari system.
        - Hundi: 
            - A financial instrument similar to a bill of exchange, used for money transfers and trade credit.
        - Jotedar: 
            - Wealthy landholders in Bengal who controlled land and competed with zamindars in revenue collection.

    - Industries in Pre-British India
        - Handicraft Industries
            - Contributed 6% to the 18th-century GDP.
            - Types of Production
                - Jajmani/Balutedar System: Artisans produced goods for local consumption.
                - Urban Artisanal Production: Focused on high-quality items for broader markets.
                - Dadni System: Merchants advanced cash to artisans for specific goods.
                - Karkhanas: Manufactured luxury items for royalty and nobility.
        - Textile Industry
            - A major industry with centers in Dhaka, Surat, Ahmedabad, and Lucknow. Famous products included muslins of Dhaka, brocades of Banaras, and chintzes from the Coromandel Coast.
        - Shipbuilding
            - India was a leading shipbuilder, with centers in Bombay, Surat, and Tuticorin. Indian ships were sturdier and cheaper than European ones, led by the Wadia Group in the 18th century.
        - Metallurgy and Other Industries
            - Wootz steel (high-grade metal) was globally prized. Other products included jute, paper, oils, and perfumes.

    - Significant Agricultural Changes
        - New Land Revenue Policies: Altered land ownership patterns.
        - Land Fragmentation: British policies caused deindustrialization, driving people into agriculture, leading to land subdivision and fragmentation.
        - Commercialization of Agriculture: Shifted focus to cash crops (crops produced for it's commercial value rather than consumption by grower), affecting food security.
        - Entry of Middlemen: Intermediaries exploited farmers, increasing their vulnerability.

    - British Land Revenue Policies
        - Background
            - In 1765, the East India Company gained the Diwani of Bengal, Bihar, and Orissa, granting them revenue collection rights. Robert Clive secured these rights but relied on Nawab's officials for indirect tax collection, who overtaxed peasants and pocketed profits, causing inefficiency.
            - Warren Hastings attempted reforms like five-year and annual settlements but achieved limited success.
            - Lord Cornwallis introduced the Permanent Settlement in 1793, redefining the landlord-tenant relationship.
        - Permanent Settlement (1793)
            - Introduced by Governor-General Cornwallis in 1790 as a decennial (10-year) settlement, made permanent in 1793, also known as the Zamindari System. Covered 19% of British India, mainly Bengal, Bihar, and Orissa.
            - Features
                - Hereditary Rights: Zamindars gained rights to sell or transfer land hereditarily.
                - Revenue Obligations: Zamindars paid 10/11th of the revenue to the government, keeping 1/11th for themselves.
                - 'Sunset Law': Set a fixed payment deadline; failure led to the auctioning of zamindari rights.
                - Landlord-Tenant Formalization: Zamindars were required to issue pattas (contracts) to tenants, specifying land area and rent.
            - Drawbacks
                - Impact on Zamindars: 
                    - High revenue targets and strict deadlines often resulted in land loss, with many estates auctioned due to non-payment.
                - Impact on Peasants: 
                    - Peasants faced oppression through property seizures, beatings, and evictions, often borrowing from moneylenders to pay rent, deepening indebtedness.
                - Impact on Agriculture: 
                    - Absentee landlordism and neglect of land care caused agricultural stagnation, disconnecting revenue demands from productivity.
            - Absentee Landlordism
                - Zamindars delegated management to middlemen and moved to urban centers like Calcutta, reinforcing feudalism and widening the rich-poor gap.
        - Ryotwari System (1820)
            - Introduction
                - Developed by Thomas Munro in 1820, first implemented by Alexander Reed in 1792 in the Madras Presidency. Ryots (cultivators) were recognized as landowners, with families providing the primary labor force, allowing direct tax collection from them. Covered 51% of British India, including Assam, Coorg, Madras, and Bombay.
            - Features
                - Direct Tax Collection: Revenue was collected directly from ryots, bypassing intermediaries.
                - Ownership Rights: Ryots received ownership, occupancy, and transfer rights without landholding limits.
                - Flexible Revenue System: Revenue adjusted based on production levels.
                - Government Control over Barren Land: Such land could be cultivated, with revenue shared between cultivators and the state.
                - Periodic Revenue Revision: Revenue agreements could be revised as circumstances changed.
            - Drawbacks
                - Overestimated Revenue: Unrealistic targets overburdened cultivators.
                - Harsh Collection Methods: Oppressive techniques were frequently used.
                - Rise of Moneylenders: High rents pushed ryots into debt traps.
                - While it eliminated intermediaries like zamindars and aimed to simplify tax collection, it led to landlessness and rural indebtedness.
        - Mahalwari System (1822)
            - Introduction
                - Conceptualized by Holt Mackenzie in 1819 and implemented by R.M. Bird in 1822 in North West territories, revised by Governor-General William Bentinck in 1833. 
                - Known as the Modified Zamindari System, it blended features of Zamindari and Ryotwari systems. Land was organized into mahals (villages) overseen by village headmen, acting as revenue units with land distributed among individual peasants who collectively paid the government.
            - Features
                - Village-Based Revenue Collection: Village headmen collected revenue, emphasizing community administration.
                - Mahal as Revenue Units: Villages were grouped into mahals for revenue assessment.
                - Fixed State Share and Regular Revisions: Initially 50%, later raised to 66%.
                - Revenue Based on Net Produce: Calculated after deducting expenses and wages.
            - Drawbacks
                - Inaccurate Records: Collectors manipulated records, skewing assessments.
                - Exorbitant Tax Assessments: High taxes led to indebtedness and land sales.
                - Undermined Authority: Replaced traditional leaders with British-appointed village heads.

        - 

    - Commercialisation of Agriculture
        - Major Cash Crops of the 18th Century
            - Silk, opium, indigo, pepper, sugarcane, and tea.
        - Results (Negative)
            - Income Instability: Exposure to global markets triggered revolts.
            - Bonded Labour: Farmers faced exploitative labor conditions.
            - Exploitation and Dependency: Middlemen and moneylenders dominated farmers.
            - Decline in Food Production: Shift to cash crops reduced food security.
        - Results (Positive)
            - Growth of Exports: Increased trade with foreign markets.
            - Special and Economic Transactions: Enhanced economic activity.
            - Regional Specialization: Areas focused on specific cash crops.

    - Deindustrialisation
        - Definition
            - Deindustrialization refers to the decline in manufacturing sector employment due to economic or political changes, evident under British rule as traditional industries weakened.
        - Causes Under British Rule
            - Import of machine-made British goods overwhelmed local production.
            - Loss of traditional Indian craft industries due to competition. Economists like R.C. Dutt and Madan Mohan Malaviya noted the flood of Manchester-made cotton cloth imports.
        - Reasons for Deindustrialization
            - Decline in Royal Patronage: 
                - Reduced demand for handmade crafts as artisans competed with machine-made goods.
            - Increase in Trade Imbalance: 
                - Raw materials exported to Britain were turned into finished goods sold back in India, replacing indigenous textiles. The Charter Act of 1813 enforced one-way free trade, taxing Indian goods while British imports entered freely.
            - Lack of Modern Industrialization: 
                - British policies neglected technological investment in Indian industries, leaving them uncompetitive.
            - Impact of Railways (Post-1853): 
                - Facilitated raw material extraction from rural areas and widespread distribution of British goods.
        - Outcomes of Deindustrialization
            - Ruralization: 
                - Artisans abandoned their trades and moved to villages, straining agriculture. Between 1881 and 1931, the farming population grew from 70 million to 100 million, while industrial workers dropped from 21 million to 13 million.
            - Collapse of Weavers and Artisans: 
                - Pre-1780s, artisans sold freely; post-1780s, gomasthas (East India Company agents) forced them to sell only to the British under the Khatbandi system, legally binding them to the Company.
            - Shift in India's Trade Dynamics: 
                - In the 18th century, cotton cloth was 81% of exports; post-deindustrialization, exports shifted to raw materials like opium and indigo, with cotton cloth falling to 3.7%, while British imports surged, reversing trade trends.
            - Legislative and Policy Impact: 
                - 19th-century policies favored British-built ships, further weakening Indian shipping.
            
- Socio-Religious Reform Movements
    - Understanding Socio-Religious Reform Movements
        - Aim
            - To transform society and religion by addressing social evils and promoting enlightenment for overall improvement.
        - Key Issues Addressed
            - Women's Emancipation (liberation): 
                - Abolition of practices like sati, infanticide, and child marriage, alongside advocacy for widow remarriage.
            - Caste Reform: 
                - Eradication of caste discrimination and untouchability to promote social equality.
            - Promotion of Education: 
                - Fostered (encouraged) societal enlightenment through widespread education initiatives.
            - Religious Reforms: 
                - Combated idolatry and polytheism, and countered exploitation by priests to purify religious practices.
        - Factors Leading to Socio-Religious Reforms
            - Contact with the West: 
                - Exposure to modern ideas of liberty, equality, and democracy influenced India's emerging middle class.
            - Christian Missionary Efforts: 
                - The Charter Act of 1813 permitted missionaries to critique Indian religious practices, sparking reformist responses.
            - Education: 
                - Indian and European scholars fostered pride in India's heritage, empowering reform movements through knowledge and awareness.
        - Reform Strategies and Approaches
            - Reforms from Within: 
                - Advocated self-initiated changes through raising societal awareness.
            - Reforms through Legislation: 
                - Sought legal solutions to social issues, though colonial support was often limited.
            - Reforms through Symbols of Change: 
                - Employed radical actions, such as those by the Derozians, to symbolize transformation.
            - Reforms through Social Work: 
                - Combined practical efforts with intellectual endeavors to effect change.

    - Types of Reformers
        - Revivalists: 
            - Aimed to restore religion to its original greatness, exemplified by movements like Wahabi and Deobandi.
        - Reformists: 
            - Reshaped society with modern ideas, as seen in organizations like Brahmo Samaj and the Aligarh movement.

    - Socio-Religious Reform Movements - Eastern India
        - Raja Ram Mohan Roy and Brahmo Samaj
            - Known as the Father of Modern India for his pioneering reform efforts.
            - Key Contributions
                - Authored “Tuhfat-ul-Muwahhidin” (A Gift to Monotheists) in Persian in 1805, promoting monotheistic ideas.
                - Founded Atmiya Sabha in 1815 to critique idolatry and polytheism, advocating monotheism and rational thought.
                - Established the Calcutta Unitarian Committee in 1823 with Dwarka Nath Tagore, uniting prominent Brahmins and European residents to promote Hindu social reforms.
                - Influenced the abolition of sati in 1829 with Governor-General William Bentinck's support, drawing on Dayabhaga and Mitakshara legal schools, and championed widow rights and women's education.
                - Supported the establishment of Hindu College in 1817 to advance education.
                - Founded Vedanta College in 1825 to blend Western and Indian sciences for a holistic education.
                - Translated Vedas and Upanishads into accessible languages and published Sambad Kaumudi (Bengali) and Mirat-ul-Akbar (Persian) to spread reformist ideas.
            - Brahmo Samaj (1828)
                - Focused on monotheism, social equality, and human dignity as core principles.
        - Evolution of Brahmo Samaj After Raja Ram Mohan Roy
            - After Roy's death in 1833, leadership passed to Debendranath Tagore, father of Rabindranath Tagore.
            - Debendranath Tagore's Contributions
                - Founded Tattvabodhini Sabha in 1839 to promote rationalism, focusing on India's history and indigenous languages, continuing Roy's legacy.
                - Worked to reform Hinduism internally by eliminating idolatry and irrational customs, while resisting Christian missionaries' critiques and conversion efforts.
                - Expanded Brahmo Samaj's influence beyond Bengal to regions like the United Provinces, Punjab, Bombay, and Madras.
            - Keshab Chandra Sen's Role
                - A prominent reformer who joined Brahmo Samaj in the mid-19th century, bringing new directions.
                - Established the Tabernacle of New Dispensation in 1867 to integrate Hinduism, Islam, and Christianity into a unified spiritual framework.
                - Founded the Indian Reform Association in 1870 to address women's rights, caste discrimination, and education.
                - His radical ideas, including support for inter-caste marriages and teachings from all religions, led to a split in 1866: Brahmo Samaj of India (led by Keshab Chandra Sen) and Adi Brahmo Samaj (led by Debendranath Tagore).
            - Sadharan Brahmo Samaj (1878)
                - Emerged after Keshab Chandra Sen conducted his underage daughter's marriage using orthodox rituals, contradicting reformist principles.
                - Disgruntled members formed this group, focusing on original Brahmo ideals: faith in a singular supreme being, emphasis on reason and morality, and opposition to orthodox practices.
                - 
        - Henry Vivian Derozio and Young Bengal Movement
            - Derozio: An Anglo-Indian radical thinker and professor at Hindu College, Kolkata, who founded the Academic Association and championed free thought, liberty, equality, and fraternity.
            - Young Bengal Movement: Influenced by Western thinkers, it promoted Raja Ram Mohan Roy's ideas, emphasizing education, press freedom, and justice.
        - Ishwar Chandra Vidyasagar
            - Key Contributions
                - Reformed Sanskrit education by admitting non-Brahmins to Sanskrit College in 1851.
                - Led the Hindu Widow Remarriage Act of 1856 to legalize widow remarriage.
                - Advocated women's education and established schools for girls.
                - Authored Barna Parichay, a foundational text for modern Bengali grammar, significantly contributing to societal reform.

    - Socio-Religious Reform Movements - Western India
        - Balshastri Jambhekar
            - Known as the "Father of Marathi Journalism" for his pioneering media work.
            - Key Contributions: Founded Darpan, the first Marathi newspaper, in 1832.
            - Social Reforms: Opposed caste discrimination, sati, and child marriage, while advocating women's education and widow remarriage.
        - Manav Dharma Sabha
            - Founded in Surat in 1844 by Durgaram Manchharam, Dadoba Pandurang, and Dalpatram Bhagubai.
            - Focus: Challenged caste systems and societal superstitions through weekly meetings discussing rationalism and equality.
        - Paramahansa Mandali
            - Established in Bombay in 1849 by Ram Bal Krishna Jayakar and Dadoba Pandurang as a radical socio-religious group.
            - Teachings: Advocated monotheism and morality over rituals, opposed caste discrimination, and supported widow remarriage and women's education.
        - Prarthana Samaj
            - Founded by Atmaram Pandurang in Bombay in 1867, influenced by Brahmo Samaj ideals.
            - Goals: Promoted women's education and the abolition of caste discrimination.
        - Jyotirao Phule and the Satyashodhak Samaj
            - Jyotirao Phule: Criticized orthodox Brahmins and caste dominance, advocating education for women and lower castes, earning the title “Mahatma” for his social reform works. Published Gulamgiri and other equality-focused writings.
            - Savitribai Phule (Wife): Opened India's first school for girls in 1851 and founded Mahila Seva Mandal in 1852.
            - Satyashodhak Samaj (1873): Promoted inter-caste marriages, elimination of child marriage, widow remarriage, and education for marginalized sections to achieve societal equality.
        - Gopal Hari Deshmukh (Lokhitwadi)
            - A prominent social reformer and writer from Maharashtra.
            - Key Contributions: Advocated modern human values and criticized social evils through his journal Prabhakar (1848-1889), supported the Swadeshi movement for self-reliance, and championed women's education while opposing caste discrimination and untouchability.
        - Gopal Ganesh Agarkar
            - Co-founded New English School (1880) in Poona, Deccan Education Society (1884), and Fergusson College (1885).
            - Advocated for the Age of Consent Act (1891) to raise the legal marriage age, worked for women's rights, opposed child marriage, and supported legislative reforms over traditional methods.
        - Maharshi Dhondo Keshav Karve
            - Known as a pioneer for widow remarriage and women's education.
            - Key Achievements: Founded Vidhwa Vivah Uttejak Mandali (1893) to encourage widow remarriage, Hindu Widows Home Association (1896) for shelter and education, and SNDT Women's University (1916), India's first women's university in Maharashtra. Advocated women's self-reliance through education and vocational training, married a widow after his first wife's death as an example, and was honored with the Bharat Ratna in 1958.
        - Servants of India Society
            - Founded by Gopal Krishna Gokhale in 1905 at Poona.
            - Core Ideals: Promoted selfless service to the underprivileged, rural communities, and tribal populations, focusing on education, sanitation, and health care reforms.
            - Gopal Krishna Gokhale: Leader of the moderate Indian National Congress faction, mentored Mahatma Gandhi, coined the phrase “What Bengal thinks today, India thinks tomorrow,” and published The Hitavada (Nagpur, 1911) to spread reformist ideas.
        - Behramji Malabari
            - A Parsi social reformer focused on women's rights.
            - Key Contributions: Advocated for the Age of Consent Act (1891) to raise the marriageable age, founded Seva Sadan (1908) for shelter, education, and vocational training for destitute women, and inspired change through writings like The Indian Muse in English Garb.
        - Pandita Ramabai
            - First Indian woman awarded “Pandita” and “Saraswati” titles by the University of Calcutta for her scholarship.
            - Reforms and Contributions: Founded Sharada Sadan (Bombay) for widows and Mukti Mission (1890s) in Pune as a Christian charity for destitute women, toured the US to raise funds for widows, authored Stri Dharma Niti (Morals for Women) in 1882 and The High-Caste Hindu Woman critiquing child marriage and widow exploitation, and received the Kaisar-i-Hind Medal in 1919.
        - Rukhmabai Case
            - Rukhmabai (1864-1955): A physician and feminist, one of India's first women doctors.
            - Legal Battle: Fought against child marriage in Rukhmabai vs. Dadaji Bhikaji (1885), refusing to live with her husband, resulting in a landmark verdict questioning child marriage legality and highlighting women's rights.
        - Social Service League
            - Founded by N.M. Joshi in Bombay in 1911 to promote social welfare.
        - Vishnushastri Chiplunkar
            - Co-founded Mahratta and Kesari newspapers with Bal Gangadhar Tilak and Gopal Ganesh Agarkar to advocate reform and nationalism.

    - Socio-Religious Reform Movements - Pan India
        - Ramakrishna Movement
            - Founded by Ramakrishna Paramahansa (1834-1886).
            - Core Teachings: All religions lead to the same goal, emphasizing spiritual equality and service to mankind as service to God.
            - Objectives: Establish a monastic order for spiritual pursuit and engage in philanthropic and charitable works across caste, color, and creed.
            - Paramahansa's disciples, led by Vivekananda, formed Ramakrishna Math in 1887 and Ramakrishna Mission in 1897, headquartered at Belur near Calcutta.
        - Swami Vivekananda (Narendra Nath Dutta)
            - Disciple of Ramakrishna Paramahansa and key Vedantic philosophy proponent.
            - Key Contributions: Represented Hinduism at the World Parliament of Religions (1893) in Chicago, elevating India's spiritual legacy, founded Ramakrishna Mission (1897) for education, health care, and social welfare, established Belur Math as its headquarters, and published Prabuddha Bharata (monthly in English) and Udbodhan (fortnightly in Bengali).
        - Swami Dayanand Saraswati and Arya Samaj
            - Founder: Swami Dayananda Saraswati (originally Mool Shankar Tiwari), established Arya Samaj in Bombay in 1875 with the slogan “Go Back to the Vedas.”
            - Ideology: Opposed idolatry, ritualism, and priesthood, advocated returning to Vedic principles as ultimate truth, opposed child marriages, and encouraged widow remarriage.
            - Key Contributions: Authored Satyarth Prakash (The Light of Truth), coined “India for Indians” in 1876 (known as Martin Luther of India), wrote Veda Bhasya and Veda Bhasya Bhumika, introduced the Shuddhi Movement for reconversion of Hindus (Ghar Wapsi), and promoted cow protection via Gokarunanidhi.
            - Post-Dayananda Era
                - Divisions: College Party (led by Lala Hansraj, Lala Lajpat Rai, and Lala Lal Chand) focused on modern education through DAV schools and colleges (e.g., DAV College, Lahore); Mahatma Party (later Gurukul Party, led by Swami Shraddhanand and Guru Datta Vidyarthi) emphasized Sanskrit and Vedic education via Kanya Mahavidyalaya for widows and Gurukuls.
                - Arya Mahila Samaj (Arya Women Society): Founded by Pandita Ramabai in 1882.
        - Indian Social Conference
            - Founded in 1887 by M.G. Ranade and Raghunath Rao as the social reform cell of the Indian National Congress, addressing caste discrimination and women's rights.
        - The Theosophical Society
            - Founded in 1875 in New York by Madame Helena Blavatsky and Colonel H.S. Olcott, shifted headquarters to Adyar, Madras, in 1882.
            - Key Tenets: Promoted brotherhood without distinction of race, creed, or gender, studied religion and philosophy concurrently, and investigated nature's hidden laws and mysticism.
            - Social Reform Work: Advocated caste abolition and untouchability eradication, founded Central Hindu School (1898) in Varanasi, which evolved into Banaras Hindu University led by Madan Mohan Malviya.
        - Radha Swami Satsang
            - Founded in 1861 by Shiv Dayal Saheb, emphasizing belief in one supreme being, spiritual equality, rejection of idol worship, and focus on moral living and ethical practices.
        - Dev Samaj
            - Founded by Shiv Narayan Agnihotri in 1887, focused on caste integration, women's education, and moral reform, with teachings compiled in Dev Shastra.
        - Bharat Dharma Mahamandal
            - Founded in 1902 in Varanasi to unify organizations like Sanatana Dharma Sabha and Dharma Maha Parishad, defending Hinduism against reformist movements like Arya Samaj and Theosophists.

    - Socio-Religious Reform Movements - Caste Reform Movements
        - Causes for the Rise of Caste Movements
            - Grievances of Educated Men: 
                - Discrimination by upper castes and challenges to centuries-old caste-based inequality.
            - Social Mobility Aspirations: 
                - Lower castes sought upward movement through Sanskritization, adopting upper-caste customs, manners, and taboos.
            - Radical Leaders: 
                - Figures like Jyotirao Phule and Periyar opposed Brahminical dominance.
            - British Interventions: 
                - The 1901 Census categorized castes hierarchically, reinforcing rigid social divisions.
        - Caste Reform Movements in South India
            - Ayya Vaikundar (Tamil Nadu)
                - Advocated equality and fraternity, abolishing caste-based discrimination.
                - Contributions: Simplified marriage customs without Sanskrit rituals, organized Sampanthi Bhojana (community meals) for diverse castes, dug common wells for lower castes, and inspired priests to apply sacred paste to all regardless of caste.
            - Ezhava Movement (Kerala)
                - Founder: Sree Narayana Guru Swamy among the Ezhava community.
                - Actions: Installed a Shivalinga in 1888 to challenge higher-caste monopoly, led Aruvippuram Kshetra Yogam (1889) for temple entry, education, and government representation for Ezhavas.
                - Legacy: Formed SNDP (Sree Narayana Dharma Paripalana Yogam) for rights to school admission, government jobs, road access, temple entry, and political representation.
            - Palli Movement (Tamil Nadu)
                - Focused on lower-caste Pallis adopting Kshatriya customs for social upliftment.
            - Vokkaliga Sangha (Mysore Region)
                - Founded in 1905 as an anti-Brahmin movement, promoting social, cultural, and educational upliftment of the Vokkaliga community.
            - Nadar Movement (Tamil Nadu)
                - Focused on toddy-tappers achieving Kshatriya status, established Nadar Mahajana Sangam in 1910.
            - Justice Movement (Madras)
                - Leaders: C.N. Mudaliar, T.M. Nair, P. Tyagaraja Chetti.
                - Demands: Greater representation in government services and politics for intermediate castes.
            - Self-Respect Movement (E.V. Ramaswamy Periyar)
                - Advocated weddings without Brahmin priests, forcible temple entry, and rejection of Manusmriti.
            - Periyar (E.V. Ramaswamy Naicker) - Tamil Nadu
                - Ideology: Rejected Brahminical culture and superstitions, promoted Dravidian leadership via Dravida Kazhagam, and advocated atheism, education, and anti-caste reforms.
                - Symbolism: Used black shirts and flags to protest caste hierarchies.
            - Vaikom Satyagraha (1924) (Kerala)
                - Leaders: K.P. Kesava, Sree Narayana Guru, T.K. Madhavan.
                - Objectives: Demanded temple entry for Dalits, supported by Gandhi, leading to state-managed temples opening to all in 1936.
        - Caste Reform Movements in Rest of India
            - Gopal Baba Walangkar (Maharashtra)
                - Founded Anarya Dosh-Parihar Mandali in 1886, challenged untouchability and caste oppression through writings like Vital Viddhwansak.
            - Kisan Faghu Bansode (Maharashtra)
                - Founded Antyaj Samaj for Dalit education, wrote plays, poems, and articles advocating emancipation.
            - Shivram Janba Kamble (Maharashtra)
                - Edited Somvanshiya Mitra, focusing on the Dalit cause, and organized India's first Dalit conference.
            - Maharshi Vitthal Ramji Shinde (Maharashtra)
                - Founded India Depressed Classes Mission in 1906, advocated untouchability eradication and Dalit upliftment, established the “All India Untouchability League.”
            - Mahad Movement
                - Led by Dr. B.R. Ambedkar in March 1927, targeting caste-based Hindu traditions. Earlier, in 1924, he founded Bahishkrit Hitkarini Sabha to voice Dalit challenges, guided by the principle “Educate, Agitate, and Organize.”

    - Parsi Reform Movements
        - Rahnomae Mazdayasanan Sabha (1851)
            - Focused on restoring Zoroastrian religion, led by Naoroji Furdonji and Dadabhai Naoroji (Rast Goftar).

    - Muslim Reform Movements
        - Wahhabi Movement (Revivalist)
            - Founder: Syed Ahmad Barelvi.
            - Advocated unity in Islamic interpretation and resistance against British rule and Sikh authority.
        - Fairaizi Movement (Revivalist)
            - Founder: Haji Shariatullah.
            - Aimed to protest un-Islamic practices among Muslims, influenced by the Wahhabi Movement.
        - Ahmadiyya Movement (Punjab) (Reformist)
            - Founder: Mirza Ghulam Ahmad in 1889, promoted universal brotherhood, Western liberal education, and religious tolerance.
        - Syed Ahmed Khan (Reformist)
            - Established Muhammadan Anglo-Oriental College in 1875 (later Aligarh Muslim University), founded the Scientific Society of Aligarh modeled on the Royal Society of London, and sought British support to modernize Muslim society through education and employment post-revolt politics.

    - Sikh Reform Movements
        - Nirankari Movement
            - Founder: Baba Dayal Das (1783-1855).
            - Rejected idol worship, emphasized Guru Nanak's teachings, and opposed evils like meat-eating and liquor consumption.
        - Namdhari Movement
            - Founder: Baba Ram Singh (1816-1885).
            - Objectives: Revitalized Sikhism and enforced strict vegetarianism.
        - Singh Sabha Movement (1873)
            - Led by Thakur Singh Sandhawalia and Giani Gian Singh, established Singh Sabha of Amritsar to counter Christian and Muslim conversion efforts, promote Gurumukhi and Punjabi literature, and founded Khalsa College in Amritsar in 1892.
        - Akali Movement
            - Targeted corrupt Mahants managing Sikh gurdwaras and aimed to restore control of Khalsa College to the Sikh community, supported the Non-Cooperation Movement, and resulted in the Sikh Gurdwara Act of 1925 for democratic management.
            

- People's Resistance Against British Rule (Revolts)
    - Meaning of People's Resistance
        - Definition
            - The term "people" refers to diverse segments of Indian society adversely affected by foreign domination, including peasants, artisans, tribals, ruling classes (both active and dispossessed), military personnel (serving under the Company or demobilized from ex-rulers' armies), and religious leaders (both Hindu and Muslim).
        - Purpose of Resistance
            - To safeguard their interests, these groups fought both independently and in collaboration with others against British rule.

    - Factors for People's Uprisings Against the British
        - Economic and Social Grievances
            - Collapse of indigenous industries due to British economic policies.
            - Exploitation by non-tribal people like moneylenders and traders.
            - Discontent stemming from oppressive land policies.
        - Impact of East India Company's Rule
            - Resentment from zamindars and artisans over lost privileges and livelihoods.
            - Erosion of traditional rights and ownership systems under Company governance.
        - Cultural Factors
            - Desire for autonomy within the Indian cultural and political framework.
            - Resistance to imposed cultural influences like Sanskritization, which threatened local identities.

    - Civil and Peasant Uprisings During British Rule (Pre-1857)
        - Sanyasi Revolt (1760s-1800)
            - Nature of Revolt
                - An uprising by Hindu Sanyasis and Muslim Fakirs in Eastern India, supported by rural impoverished communities and minor zamindars.
            - Causes
                - Religious Restrictions: British restrictions on pilgrimage and taxes on religious donations angered spiritual leaders.
                - Economic Hardship: The Bengal Famine of 1770 worsened conditions, fueling rebellion.
            - Major Areas
                - Purnea, Malda, Dhaka, Rangpur, Cooch Behar, and Murshidabad in Bengal Province.
            - Notable Leaders and Activities
                - Leaders included Bhawani Pathak, Majnu Shah, Chirag Ali, and Musa Shah, who launched surprise raids on British outposts, treasuries, and factories.
            - Outcome
                - Eventually suppressed after a prolonged struggle by British forces.
            - Significance
                - Inspired literary works like "Devi Chaudhurani" and "Anandamath" by Bankim Chandra Chatterjee, reflecting its cultural legacy.
        - Rangpur Dhing (1783)
            - Area of Revolt
                - Rangpur District of Bengal in 1783.
            - Nature of Revolt
                - A protest by peasants and zamindars against excessive revenue demands under the Ijaradari System (land auctioned to the highest bidder).
            - Leader
                - Kena Sarkar.
            - Outcome
                - Highlighted flaws in the Ijaradari System, contributing to reforms like the Permanent Settlement.
        - Paika Rebellion (1817)
            - Location
                - Odisha.
            - Causes
                - Resistance against British policies, particularly high land revenue demands.
            - Leaders
                - Bakshi Jagabandhu Bidyadhar and Mukunda Deva.
            - Outcome
                - Suppressed by 1818 but led to governance changes, including large remission of arrears, reduced assessments, and a new settlement on fixed tenures.
            - Additional Fact
                - In 2017, Odisha urged recognition as the First War of Independence, though the Indian Council of Historical Research (ICHR) disagreed.
            - Who Were Paikas?
                - Paikas were foot soldiers recruited since the 16th century by Odisha kings for martial services in exchange for hereditary rent-free lands.
        - Mysore Rebellion (1830)
            - Location
                - Mysore (Nagar District).
            - Causes
                - Exploitative taxation after the Wodeyar dynasty's reinstatement and enforcement of subsidiary alliances, burdening peasants and lower societal sections.
            - Leader
                - Sardar Malla.
            - Outcome
                - Resulted in direct British administration of the region.
        - Kuka Movement (1840-1850)
            - Nature
                - Also known as the Namdhari Movement, it aimed at religious purification and political resistance against British rule.
            - Founder
                - Bhagat JawaharMal.
            - Leader
                - Baba Ram Singh, who emphasized military training and structured resistance; Kukas boycotted British goods, institutions, and laws.
            - Outcome
                - Suppressed by the British; Baba Ram Singh was deported to Rangoon.

    - Peasant Movements with Religious Overtones
        - Narkelberia Uprising (1830s)
            - Leader
                - Syed Mir Nisar Ali (Titu Mir).
            - Nature
                - Muslim tenants rose against Hindu landlords and British indigo planters imposing a bread tax on Faraizi Muslims; initially economic, it merged into the Wahabi movement with religious overtones.
        - The Pagal Panthis (1825-1833)
            - Nature
                - Peasant uprisings led by a religious sect in present-day Bangladesh, initiated by Karam Shah as a religious movement, later turned political under his son Tipu with local tribes.
            - Activities
                - Directed against zamindar oppression, refusing excessive rent and attacking their houses.
            - Outcome
                - Suppressed by British large-scale military operations.
        - Faraizi Revolt (1818-1857)
            - Leaders
                - Haji Shariatullah, later Dudu Mian of the Faraizi sect.
            - Nature
                - Sought to cleanse Islam and support tenants' rights against zamindars; many Faraizis joined the Wahhabi Movement.

    - 

    - Tribal Uprisings During British Rule
        - Overview
            - Tribes in the 18th century lived in isolation with distinct agricultural methods, traditions, and societal structures, mainly in forested lands encroached upon by the British, leading to recurrent uprisings.
            - 
        - Understanding the Evolution of Tribal Movements Under British Rule
            - First Phase (1795-1860)
                - Rise of the British Empire; leadership from traditional sections like zamindars whose privileges were undermined.
            - Second Phase (1860-1920)
                - Intensive colonialism and mercantilism impacted tribal economy, land, and forests; leadership emerged from lower tribal ranks.
            - Third Phase (1920-1947)
                - Tribals engaged in nationalist, agrarian, and separatist movements; leadership from educated tribal leaders and outsiders.
            - 
        - Plight of the Tribals Under Colonial Rule - Key Issues
            - Debt Spirals and Outsider Dominance
                - Traders and moneylenders offered loans for non-local goods, leading to dependency, high interest rates, and debt cycles.
            - Eroded Chieftaincy (From Leaders to Enforcers)
                - Tribal leaders reduced to enforcers of British policies, losing respect among their people.
            - The Agrarian Dilemma (Forced Settlements and Fallow Fields)
                - Forced settlement and farming under unsuitable conditions increased hardships.
            - Forests Under Siege (Land Now State Property of British)
                - Forest laws restricted access, impacting livelihoods, forcing relocation, and reducing autonomy.
            - Labour Traps (Plantations and Mines)
                - Recruitment for tea plantations and mining led to loss of mobility and freedom.
        - Indian Forest Act
            - Background
                - A key conflict source due to timber demand in the 1850s for railways (fuel and sleepers) and shipbuilding (Indian teak for British naval dominance).
            - Forest Policy (1864)
                - Established by Lord Dalhousie, declaring forests state property and enabling British control over tree-containing land.
            - Forest Act of 1878
                - Divided forests into Reserved (fully government-controlled for heavy commercial use), Protected (partly government-controlled), and Village Forests (controlled by nearby villages); tribes lost traditional rights like grazing and agriculture, receiving only limited privileges.
            - Forest Act of 1927
                - Did not acknowledge community rights; individuals or communities had to claim rights before a Forest Officer; traditional practices like shifting agriculture and collecting forest produce were made illegal.

    - Revolt by Mainland Indian Tribes
        - Pahariyas' Rebellion (1778)
            - Area
                - Rajmahal Hills (Jharkhand).
            - Cause
                - Protest against British disruption of their lifestyle.
            - Main Tactic
                - Engaged British in guerrilla warfare.
            - Result
                - British declared the Pahariya region “Daman-i-Koh” (forbidden territory), agreeing not to extend administration further.
        - Chuar Uprising (1798-1799)
            - Area
                - Midnapore and Bankura districts of Bengal.
            - Cause
                - Protest against British revenue policies.
            - Activities
                - Multiple waves between 1766 and 1798, led by Durjan Singh, a deprived zamindar; also known as the Revolt of Jungle Mahal; brutally suppressed by British.
        - Kol Revolt (1831)
            - Location
                - Chotanagpur.
            - Cause
                - Against land transfers to non-tribal settlers and oppressive British colonial policies.
            - Leader
                - Budho Bhagat, under whom rebels killed about a thousand perceived oppressors.
            - Outcome
                - Suppressed with large-scale military operations.
        - Ho and Munda Uprisings (1820-1837)
            - Location
                - Singhbhum and broader Chotanagpur region.
            - Cause
                - Resistance to British revenue systems and Bengali settlers.
            - Leadership
                - Raja of Parahat.
            - Activities
                - Ho Revolt persisted until 1827 when Ho tribals surrendered; in 1831, they rebelled again with Mundas, subdued by 1837.
        - Koli Uprising (1822-1829)
            - Location
                - Maharashtra (particularly Akola hills).
            - Cause
                - British interventions in tribal rights and exploitation by moneylenders and zamindars.
            - Leader
                - Ramji Bhangre.
            - Activities
                - Rebels targeted Baniya moneylenders, attacking and looting financial documents; British responded with military force.
        - Ramosi Uprisings (1820s-1830s)
            - Location
                - Western Ghats.
            - Cause
                - Protest against livelihood loss after Maratha territory annexation.
            - Leaders
                - Chittur Singh and Umaji Naik.
            - Activities
                - Armed attacks on British establishments; brutally suppressed by British.
        - Santhal Rebellion (1855-1856)
            - Area
                - Santhal Pargana (Jharkhand and West Bengal).
            - Causes
                - Encroachment by outsiders (Dikus) invading land rights, economic exploitation by zamindars, and loss of traditional lifestyle.
            - Leaders
                - Sidhu and Kanhu Murmu, who rallied Santhals, believing they were divinely chosen to end oppression and restore their way of life.
            - Tactics
                - Armed insurrection with traditional weapons.
            - Outcome
                - Suppressed by British; Santhal Pargana created as an administrative district in 1855 for some autonomy; the 1876 Santhal Parganas Tenancy Act prohibited land transfer to non-tribals.
        - Khond Uprisings (1837-1856)
            - Causes
                - Resentment against British control, cultural disruption, loss of territorial rights, abolition of the Meriah System (human sacrifices for fertility), and economic exploitation via taxes and moneylenders.
            - Regions
                - Hilly tracts of Odisha and Andhra Pradesh.
            - Leader
                - Chakra Bisoi, uniting tribes like Ghumusar and Kalahandi against British policies.
            - Methods
                - Used traditional weapons like tangi (axes), bows, arrows, and swords.
            - Outcome
                - Suppressed due to superior British military strength.
        - Koya Revolts (1803-1886)
            - Causes
                - Oppression by police and moneylenders, exploitation under British forest laws.
            - Notable Leaders
                - Tomma Sora (1879-1880 rebellion) and Raja Anantayyar.
            - Methods
                - Guerrilla tactics exploiting terrain.
            - Outcome
                - Suppressed by British forces.
        - Bhil Revolts
            - Causes
                - Famine, economic hardship, and British misgovernance, with revolts in 1817, 1825, 1831, 1846, and 1913.
            - Regions
                - Western Ghats, southern Rajasthan (Banswara district), and Dhule district (Maharashtra), strategically linking central India to the western coast.
            - Notable Events
